MATTERS RAISED WITH PERMISSION  
Need to ensure participation of Me
mbers of Parliament in Government programmes 
as per the Warrant of Precedence and DoPT guidelines 
Ǜी जावेद अली खान (उǄर Ģदेश):  माननीय सभापित जी, सांसदȗ और अन्य गणमान्य Ëयिƪयȗ 
के साथ सरकार का Ëयवहार Warrant of Precedence और DoPT की गाइडलाइंस के तहत तय 
होता है, संचािलत होता है।  DoPT की गाइडलाइंस मȂ ÎपÍट उÊलेख है िक सरकारी आयोजनȗ मȂ 
उस क्षेतर् के सांसदȗ को अिनवायर् रूप से आमंितर्त िकया जाएगा। लेिकन सर, हमारे उǄर Ģदेश के 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
9

अंदर DoPT की गाइडलाइंस का रǄी भर भी अनुसरण नहीं हो रहा है। वहां जो सरकारी कायर्कर्म 
होते हȅ, उनके  अंदर भारतीय जनता पाटीर् के लोग िवराजमान रहते हȅ और िवपक्षी दलȗ के, 
खासतौर से समाजवादी पाटीर् के सांसदȗ और िवधायकȗ को एक भी कायर्कर्म मȂ नहीं बुलाया जाता 
है। मेरे जनपद, संभल मȂ दो सांसदȗ का क्षेतर् है, दोनȗ समाजवादी पाटीर् के हȅ, मȅ भी वहीं से हूँ और 
वहां चार मȂ से तीन िवधायक समाजवादी पाटीर् के हȅ। वहां िजला मुख्यालय का उǇाटन करने 
मुख्य मंतर्ी गए, लेिकन एक भी समाजवादी पाटीर् के सांसद और िवधायक को आमंितर्त नहीं िकया 
गया। सर, मुरादाबाद मȂ हवाई अƿे का उǇाटन हुआ, हालांिक वह हवाई अƿा चल नहीं रहा है। 
Ģधान मंतर्ी जी ने उसका उǇाटन िकया था। वहां सपा का सांसद है। वहाँ के 6 िवधायक मȂ 
समाजवादी पाटीर् के 5 िवधायक हȅ, िजनमȂ से िकसी को भी कायर्कर्म मȂ आमंितर्त नहीं िकया गया।  
मेरे बगल के िजले, कासगंज मȂ सपा के सांसद और िवधायकȗ को नहीं बुलाया गया। Ģयागराज मȂ 
Ģधान मंतर्ी जी गए और 5,500 करोड़ की योजनाओं का उǇाटन करके  आए, लेिकन वहां भी 
िकसी भी िवपक्षी सांसद या िवधायक को नहीं बुलाया गया। सर, मȅ आपसे यह जानना चाहता हूं 
िक क्या िवपक्षी दलȗ के सांसदȗ और िवधायकȗ को सरकारी कायर्कर्मȗ मȂ न बुलाए जाने का फैसला 
Ģधान मंतर्ी और मुख्य मंतर्ी की सहमित से िलया जाता है, या िफर कुछ अिधकारी, Åयूरोकर्ेट  और

ﮩﻤﺖѧѧѧیﺳ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﺍیﻝ ﺎѧѧѧѧﺟﺎﺗ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﺍی ﺮѧѧѧѧѧﭘﻬ ﻬѧѧѧѧ ѧﮐﭽ ﺎﺭیﺍﺩﻫѧѧѧѧ،یﮐ ﺮیﺏѧѧѧѧٹیﻭﺭﻭﮐ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺎﻩѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻧﻮﮐﺮﺷ ﺮﮐﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧیﺳ

Guidelines ںیﻡ ﺁپ ﻮﺩﻫﻦѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳﻨﺸ ﺮﻭﺍѧѧѧѧﮐ ۔ےیﺝیﺩ ﮨﺎںی ﮨﻤﺎﺭے  ﺳﺎﻣﺎﺟﮏ ﮯیﻥѧѧѧѧﺍﺋ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺎیﺍﺩﻫѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺎﺭﺗ ﺘﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧ،یﻣﻨ

ںیﻡ ﮯѧѧѧﻧ ﻮѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﮑ ﻮﻥѧѧѧѧѧﻓ ﺮﮐﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍیﺑﺘ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻣﺠﻬﮯ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﻼѧѧѧѧѧﺍیﺑ ،ﺍیگ ﺭےیﻡ ﺪѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳﺎﻧﺴ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﻼѧѧѧѧѧﺍیﺑ

SHRI SADANAND MHALU SHET TANAVADE (Goa) : Hon’ble Chairman, Sir, I rise to 
draw the attention of this august Hous e to an important matter concerning the 
ongoing widening and upgradation of NH-66  – the vital corridor connecting Mumbai 
and Goa. This highway serves as the primary road link between the Konkan region of 
Maharashtra and Goa and plays a crucial role  in tourism, trade, transportation of 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
11

goods and overall regional connectivity and development. This project of a modern 
four-lane highway corridor was sanctioned more than a decade ago with the objective 
of improving connectivity, enhancing road  safety, reducing travel time between 
Mumbai and Goa and making the journey smoot her and faster for lakhs of travelers, 
tourists and daily commuters. The completion of this highway will also greatly benefit 
the transportation of goods. Faster and smoother freight movement will support 
industries, agriculture, fisheries and sm all businesses across the Konkan belt and 
Goa. However, despite its importance, the project has faced repeated delays due to 
land acquisition issues, environmental clea rances, design modifications, contractor-
related challenges and traffic management co nstraints. As a result, several stretches 
continue to face bottlenecks, incomplete bridges, unfinishe d bypasses and uneven 
road conditions. The situation becomes pa rticularly severe during the monsoon 
season. Heavy rainfall further worsens already incomplete stretches, leading to traffic 
congestion and increased risk of accidents.  This causes immense hardship to daily 
commuters and poses serious safety concerns  for all road users. Given the strategic 
and economic importance of this highway an d approaching monsoon, it is imperative 
that the project be completed at the earliest on a priority and time-bound basis. 
Hon’ble Chairman, Sir, I, therefore, request the Government to take immediate 
steps to expedite the remaining work on NH-66, ensure better coordination among all 
stakeholders and provide a clear timeline for its early completion, especially before the 
onset of the upcoming monsoon. Thank you.

Concern over the discrimination faced by marginalized communities in JNU 
SHRI DIGVIJAYA SING H (Madhya Pradesh) : Hon’ble Chairman, Sir, I rise to raise a 
matter of urgent public importance regarding growing concerns about the 
discrimination and weakening of social just ice commitments at JNU. It is one of the 
12
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
top ten universities in the National Instit utional Ranking Framework. And two of our 
eminent alumni are sitting in the Treasury Benches now. 
Sir, JNU was established to advance na tional integration, social justice, 
democratic values in a scientif ic approach to societal challenges. In this context, the 
recent media remarks by the Vice-Chancellor who reportedly described assertions of 
historically marginalized communities as  a form of perman ent victimhood and 
suggested that such realities are being manufactured, run contrary to the very 
principles for which university was established. Sir, the statement raised concerns 
about institution’s sensitivity to caste discri mination, equity and constitutional values.  
According to a Report by the JNU Teachers’ Association, the average number of 
SC/ST students declined by around 25 per cent in just three years. Similar issues 
have been flagged in the co ntext of faculty recruitment.  Of 326 vacancies for which 
the Selection Committee was constituted, mo re than 40 reportedly resulted in 
candidates being declared ‘Not Found Suitab le’. It has been pointed out that the 
majority of these posts were reserved positions and the proportion of such outcomes 
appears to be increasing. Further, delays in faculty promotions have also been noted. 
There are 89 pending promotional cases in JNU, of which 62 have exceeded in the 
stipulated processing period. Such delays  adversely affect ca reer progression and 
Ph.D. supervision capacity. 
In view of these concerns, I urge the Government to take necessary steps to 
ensure strict adherence to reservation norms and to protect the constitutional 
commitment to inclusivity and social justice in JNU and other Central Universities. 
Thank you.

Concern over commercialization of healthcare and exploitation by  
private hospitals 
SHRI SANT BALBIR SINGH (Punjab) : & Hon’ble Chairman Sir, I thank you for giving 
me this opportunity. Sat Sri Akal to all th e Hon’ble Members present in this House. 
Sir, in our country, doctors have traditionally been accorded the status of God, and 
medical services have always been regard ed as sacred. However, it is deeply 
concerning that healthcare today is increasingly being commercialized and treated as 
a profit-making enterprise. Private hospitals have become prohibitively expensive and 
are beyond the reach of the common man. At the same time, insurance companies 
lure citizens into purchasing policies through tall claims and assurances of 
comprehensive support during medical em ergencies—moments when individuals are 
most vulnerable and in need of care. However, when claims are actually made, 
patients are often denied benefits on the basis of complex, technical, and fine-print 
terms and conditions. These clauses are de liberately drafted in a manner that is 
difficult for an ordinary person to understan d, resulting in exploitation and financial 
distress for consumers. Sir, I urge the Government to take immediate steps to 
regulate both private hospitals and insura nce companies, and to put an end to the 
exploitation of the common ma n. There is an urgent need to plug systemic loopholes 
and ensure transparency and accountability in this sector. Further, I strongly 
recommend that all policy terms and conditio ns must be written in simple language, 
preferably in the mother to ngue of the consumer, so that individuals can fully 
understand their rights and obligations and protect themselves from unfair practices. 
Sir, today, patients are increasingly being treated as mere commodities for profit, with 
private hospitals and insurance companies often acting in collusion, thereby 
compromising access to quality and a ffordable healthcare. Due to such 
circumstances, patients from nearly eight st ates are compelled to seek treatment at 
PGI Chandigarh, leading to excessive burden on the institution and immense hardship 
for patients and their families. Therefore,  I demand that another super-specialty 
hospital be established in this region of Punjab to reduce the load on PGI and improve 
accessibility to quality healthcare. It is the responsibility of the Government to ensure 
adequate medical infrastructure for its ci tizens. At the same time, I would like to 
inform this august House with a sense of pr ide that Punjab has become the first state 
to provide free medical treatment up to ₹10 lakh through empanelled hospitals. I urge 
that such a progressive scheme be replicated across other states for the benefit of the 
wider population. Thank you, Sir.

Demand for establishing Indian Institute of Information Technology (IIIT) in 
Puducherry 
SHRI S. SELVAGANABATHY (Puducherry) : Hon’ble Chairman, Sir, I rise to demand 
establishment of an Indian Institute of Info rmation Technology in the Union Territory of 
Puducherry. Puducherry has made remarkable achievements in the field of education. 
With 61.5 per cent Gross Enrolment Rati o (GER), Puducherry has the second-
highest GER in higher education in India, which means that more than six out of every 
ten young people in Puducherry are enrolled  in higher education. It is a shining 
example of aspiration of a society deeply committed to learning. Yet, despite this 
achievement, Puducherry stands deprived of premier national institutions of its own 
like IIT, IIM, IIIT or IISER. Puducherry has Institutions of Excellence like the National 
Institute of Technology, the Jawaharlal Institute of Postgraduate Medical Education 
and Research and Pondicherry University. Recently, the IIT Madras Research and 
Development Center has also been established here. It is the pride of Puducherry that 
the first Krishi Vigyan Kendra was established in 1974. These are commendable 
steps, but they are not enough to match Puducherry's potential. Therefore, I place 
before the House two specific proposals. The first one is estab lishing an Indian 
Institute of Information Technology in Puducherry. It would not only serve local 
students, but also attract talent from ac ross the country, creating a hub for digital 
innovation in South India.  Alternatively, a Central Institute of Marine Sciences may be 
established. Puducherry with its long co astline and fishing community is uniquely 
positioned to lead India in marine resear ch, ocean studies and sustainable fisheries. 
Such an Institute would strengthen our blue  economy aligned with national priorities 
and private livelihoods rooted in science and sustainability.  Further, I urge the 
Government to expand the PM SHRI school s in Puducherry. At present, there are 
only 12 PM SHRI schools across the Union territory. I request that the number of PM 
SHRI schools be increased to at least 30 in  all the four regions of Puducherry. By 
establishing an IIIT, a Central Institute of Marine Sciences, and by expanding PM 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]

SHRI schools will not only honour Puduche rry's contributions, but also invest in 
India's future. These steps will ensure that Puducherry continues to be a ...

Demand for the establishment of a dedicated Yoga University in Puri 
SHRI SUBHASISH KHUNTIA (Odisha) : Jai Jagannath! Sir, I thank you for giving me 
an opportunity to raise a matter of gr eat national importance regarding the 
establishment of a dedicated Yoga  University in Puri, Odisha. सबसे पहले 'उत्कल 
िदवस' के अवसर पर मȅ अपनी तरफ से और ओिडशावािसयȗ की तरफ से आपको और सभी 
मान्यवर सांसदȗ को शुभेच्छा और अिभनंदन ज्ञापन करता हूँ।  Sir, Puri is not just a city. It is 
one of the India’s revered spiritual centres. This sacred land has for centuries nurtured 
traditions of spirituality, discipline, and ho listic living that resonate deeply with the 
philosophy of yoga. India has contributed immensely to the world in science, 
medicine, culture, heritage, and one of it s greatest gifts is yoga. Practised since 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
29

 
 
ancient times, yoga has now spread acro ss the globe and emerged as a powerful 
means of promoting physical health, ment al well-being and inner balance, truly 
reflecting the idea that health is wealth . Yoga today enjoys global recognition, 
particularly after the adoption of the Inte rnational Day of Yoga. However, to sustain 
this momentum, there is a need to strengthen research, expand academic 
engagement and encourage greater participation of students and teachers.  There are 
about 25 yoga universities and more than  430 yoga colleges in our country and, 
Odisha, which has been one of the pioneer in maintain ing the Indian knowledge 
system, is in need to have one university which can help in spreading the knowledge 
and popularity of yoga. Sir, establishing su ch an institution in Puri would strengthen 
India’s soft power, promote wellness tour ism, generate employment and create a 
global center for yogic learning. Sir, this university would also serve as a center for 
scientific inquiry, integrating sports medi cine, mental health and lifestyle disease 
management. 
I urge the Government to take immediate steps to establish a yoga university in 
Puri, befitting its spiritual heritage and nati onal importance. If yoga is India's message 
to the world, Puri must become its gl obal classroom. Jai Jagannath. Vande Utkal 
Janani!

Urgent need for welfare measures and social security for Theyyam and Traditional 
Folk Artists 
SHRIMATI P.T. USHA (Nominated) : Hon’ble Chairman, Sir, I rise today with a deep 
sense of responsibility to speak about a community that has preserved our heritage 
with devotion, discipline and silent sacr ifice - the performers of the Theyyam.  
Coming from Kerala, I have closely witnessed the spirit behind this sacred art.  In 
regions like Kannur, Kasaragod and Kozhikod, Theyyam is not merely a performance, 
30
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
it is a faith, it is an identity and living tradition passed down through generations. But, 
behind these vibrant costumes and divine rituals lies, lives filled with struggle. Most 
Theyyam artists belong to economically w eaker sessions and their livelihood depends 
on short seasonal window. For the rest of  the year, they face uncertainty, financial 
hardship, lack of healthcare and limited opportunities for their families. As someone 
who has risen from humble beginnings, I understand the value of support, opportunity 
and dignity. These artists deserve not symp athy but security, recognition and respect 
for the noble endeavor they undertake with devotion. The time has come to move 
beyond and take meaningful action. 
I humbly urge the Government to cons ider a dedicated welfare fund for 
Theyyam performers, a monthly pension sche me for aged and retired artists, off-
season financial assistance to ensure li velihood stability, comprehensive health 
insurance considering the physical risks th ey endure, educational support for their 
children, including opportunities for high er education and entr epreneurship. These 
are not merely proposals, they are essential interventions to protect the living cultural 
heritage of our nation. At the same time, I would like to emphasize that similar 
traditional and folk artists across different States of India who preserve their unique 
cultural expressions with equal educatio n face the same challenges of financial 
insecurity, lack of social protection and limited recognition. As we move forward in the 
Amrit Kaal towards building a Viksit Bharat by 2047, under the visionary leadership of 
our hon’ble Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Mo di, it is our collective responsibility to 
ensure that development is not only econo mic but also cultural where every artist, 
every tradition and every voi ce valued and protected. In th is context, I would like to 
recollect the Sanskrit verse “संÎकृ ितः राÍटर्Îय आत्मा” means culture is the soul of 
nation. Hon’ble  Chairman, Sir, at this  moment, I would also like to remember 
Swargeeya Anoop Nittoor from Nadapuram in Kozh ikode, a gifted Theyyam artist of 
Malabar who tragically lost his life in th e Western Ghats while striving to protect 
nature. His life reminds us that these artists are not merely performers, they are 
guardians of culture, protectors of natu re and symbols of resilience. Let their 
sacrifices not go unnoticed. Thank you, Sir.

ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS 
Drug de-addiction and rehabilitation centre 
#*391. SHRI PRADIP KUMAR VARMA: Will the Minister of SOCIAL JUSTICE AND 
EMPOWERMENT be pleased to state:- 
(a)   the number of drug de-addiction an d rehabilitation centres operating in the 
country; 
                   
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
39

 
 
(b) the number of persons who benefi tted from treatment and rehabilitation 
during the last three years; 
(c) the total Central assistance released for the drug de-addiction programme ; 
and 
(d)  the targets set for the expansion of de-addiction efforts in the coming 
years? 
THE MINISTER FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE AND EMPOWERMENT (DR. VIRENDRA 
KUMAR): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a) Presently, 768 treatment and rehabilitation centers are being supported 
under National Action Plan for Drug Demand Reduction (NAPDDR) Scheme by this 
Department across the country. These includ e 348 Integrated Rehabilitation Centres 
for Addicts (IRCAs), 76 Outreach and Dr op-in Centres (ODICs), 45 Community-
Based Peer-Led Interventions (CPLIs), 145 District De-Addiction Centres (DDACs) 
and 154 Addiction Treatment Facilities (ATFs). 
 (b) The number of persons who benefi tted from treatment and rehabilitation 
during the last three years under NAPDDR are as follows- 
Financial Year No. of Beneficiaries 
2022-23 3,58,134 
2023-24 6,06,167 
2024-25 8,20,832 
Total 17,85,133 
 (c) The total Central assistance releas ed for the drug de-addiction programme 
under NAPDDR during the last 3 years is as follows:- 
Financial Year Funds Released (Rs. in Crore) 
2022-23 97.51 
2023-24 172.08 
2024-25 266.69 
Total 536.28 
40
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 (d) Expansion of de-addiction efforts is a continuous process.  There are 
provisions for establishment of treatment and rehabilitation centres for individuals 
affected by substance use under the Scheme . In this spirit, the Ministry aims to 
establish at least one centre in every dist rict, while also envisaging dedicated de-
addiction facilities for women, for children,  and for individuals in prison settings, 
based on demand from States/UTs. Th ese centres provide comprehensive and 
holistic recovery and rehabilitation services. 
 In addition to strengthening accessible treatment facilities, the scheme 
emphasizes preventive and awareness in itiatives through community awareness 
campaigns, school and college based prev ention programs, capacity building of 
stakeholders, and promotion of healthy lifes tyles to reduce the risk of substance use 
among youth, women, and the broader community.   Mass awareness programmes 
and activities are being conducted under the NashaMukt Bharat Abhiyaan to reduce 
the drug demand in the country.

Governments and UTs administrations. Fund s are allocated to the States and UTs 
under various components based on the pr oposals submitted by them during Annual 
Programme Implementation Plan (APIP) meet ings and their eligibility to receive funds 
as per schematic norms.

From Financial Year 2021-22, funds are released through PFMS in compliance 
with the Single Nodal Agency (SNA) guid elines issued by the Department of 
Expenditure. Release of central funds to State Governments and UT Administration is 
subject to compliance of SNA norms by the States/UTs which, inter-alia, include 
timely transfer of matching State share, de posit of interest to Consolidated Fund of 
India and at least 75% expenditure of the previous grant-in-aid released to the 
States/UTs. 
Further, SNA SPARSH module which is based on just in time method, has 
been made applicable w.e.f 01.11.2025, for Mission Saksham Anganwadi and Poshan 
2.0. Under this module, mother sanctions are issued to the States and UTs with 
Legislature. The release of funds is based on the expenditure reported by the States 
and UTs in their Utilisation Certificates (UC) and State of Expenditure (SoE) and their 
admissibility as per schematic norms. 
Central funds under Mission Saksham An ganwadi and Poshan 2.0 to the State 
of Tamil Nadu have been released in ti mely manner based on compliance of norms 
under SNA and SNA SPARSH modules by the State. The component wise amount of 
funds released and amount of mother sanc tion issued to the State of Tamil Nadu

In addition to this, Mission Shakti ai ms at strengthening interventions for 
women safety, security and empowerment. Mi ssion Shakti comprises of two verticals 
‘Sambal’ for safety and security of wo men and ‘Samarthya’ for empowerment of 
women. The ‘Sambal’ has components of One Stop Centre (OSC), Women Helpline 
(WHL), Beti Bachao Beti Padhao (BBBP ) and Nari Adalat (Nari Adalat). The 
‘Samarthya’ has components of Shakti Sa dan, Sakhi Niwas, Padhan Mantri Matru 
Vandana Yojana (PMMVY), Palna and SANKALP: Hub for Empowerment of Women 
(SANKALP: HEW). 
As per the Mission Shakti guidelines, these schemes are Centrally Sponsored 
and the Central Government provides 100% financial assistance to States/ UTs. The 
overall implementation of these scheme s lies with the State Governments/UT 
Administrations. 
The Ministry of Women and Child Develo pment has introduced Palna Scheme 
under the Samarthya Vertical of Umbrella Missi on Shakti for all States and UTs w.e.f. 
01 April 2022. 
Anganwadi centres are the world's larges t childcare institutions dedicated to 
providing essential care and support to childre n ensuring delivery of  care facilities till 
the last mile. In a first of its kind appr oach, Ministry has extended the services of 
childcare through Anganwadi cum Cr èche (AWCC). Anganwadi cum Cr èche 
initiative aims to increase ‘women work force participation’ in the economy. The 
objective of the scheme is to provide quality crèche facility in safe and secure 
environment for children (from ages 6 months  – 6 years), nutritional support, health 
and cognitive development of children, growth monitoring and immunization. Crèche 
facilities under Palna are prov ided to all mothers, irresp ective of their employment 
status. 
Palna scheme is a Centrally Sponsore d Scheme. Programme Approval Board 
(PAB) meetings are held with the Stat e Governments and UT Administrations 
annually wherein States and UTs submit  proposals for open ing Anganwadi-cum-
Creches and operationalize them. State Go vernments and UT Administrations also 
contribute their corresponding share for the implementation of the Scheme. A total of 
600 AWCCs have been approved by the Mi nistry for State of Tamil Nadu as per 
proposals received from them. 
Amount of funds raised by the State sh ould be as per schematic guidelines. 
For just-in-time release of funds, SNA Spar sh has been introduced for Palna Scheme

placement rates for ITI graduates; and 
(e) the steps taken to promote dual-syste m training where students divide their 
learning seamlessly between the ITI and the factory floor?

[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
THE MINISTER OF STATE OF THE MI NISTRY OF SKILL DEVELOPMENT AND 
ENTREPRENEURSHIP (SHRI JAYANT CHAUDHAR Y): (a) to (e) A statement is laid 
on the table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a) and (b)  The Government has launched Pradhan Mantri Skilling and 
Employability Transformation through Up graded ITIs (PM-SETU) scheme for the 
infrastructure upgradation of Industrial Tr aining Institutes (ITIs) across the country 
including the State of Uttar Pradesh. The scheme envisages to enhance the overall 
quality and relevance of vocational traini ng with upgraded labs, machines, and 
industry-aligned curriculum in the country. The Scheme has a total outlay of Rs. 
60,000 crores (Central Share – Rs. 30,00 0 crore, State Share – Rs. 20,000 crore 
and Industry Share – Rs. 10,000 crore). 
The key objectives of the scheme are: 
i. To improve the quality of training delivery in ITIs and National Skill Training      
 Institutes (NSTIs); 
ii. To modernize infrastructure and equipment as per industry standards; 
iii.  To introduce industry-aligned long-term and short-term courses, especially 
 in new and emerging sectors; 
iv.  To strengthen industry linkage for demand-driven skilling and better 
 employment outcomes; and 
v. To enhance the capacity of National Skill Training Institutes (NSTIs) for  
 training of trainers. 
The scheme comprises of two components: 
Component I – Upgradation of 1,000 Government ITIs (200 Hub ITIs and 800 
Spoke ITIs) in a Hub and Spoke model wherein upgradation includes smart 
classrooms, modern labs, digital content, and new courses aligned to industry 
needs. 
Component II – Capacity Augmentation of five NSTIs located in Bhubaneswar, 
Chennai, Hyderabad, Kanpur, and Ludhiana, including setting-up sector-
specific National Centres of Excellence for skilling, with focus on advanced 
training of trainers with global partnership. 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
55

 
 
Under the PM-SETU scheme, industry pa rticipation in curriculum design, 
training delivery and apprenticeship opportunities is strengthened through an 
industry-led governance model. The indust ry-led Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) is 
provided the necessary autonomy to prop ose modifications in the curriculum by 
introducing new courses and upgrading existing courses as per industry 
requirements. The scheme focuses on im proving employability outcomes through 
industry-led governance and aims to enable  learners to acquire industry-relevant 
skills, gain exposure to real work environments, and access career guidance, 
placement support and self-employment opportunities through strengthened 
institutional systems and industry linkages. 
Upgradation of curricula for ITI training programmes is a continuous process 
and is generally undertaken every three ye ars. To meet the demands of the modern 
economy and emerging technologies in the industrial sector, new courses are 
continuously developed and existing cour ses are upgraded in consultation with 
industry, academia and States/UTs. The courses are updated by incorporating new 
technologies and the latest tools, equipm ent and machinery in the curricula, keeping 
in view the requirements of emerging technologies. During the last three years, 14 
Craftsmen Training Scheme (CTS) courses, 15 short-term courses and 07 Flexi-MoU 
courses have been develo ped and approved by National Council for Vocational 
Education and Training (NCVET). Addition ally, 22 courses have been revised and 
approved to further align training with in dustry requirements. The list is attached at 
Annexure-I. 
Under CTS, 31 New Age Courses, in cluding Green Hydrogen Production 
Technician and Internet of Things Technician, have been intr oduced in ITIs. The 
enrolment of trainees in ITIs across the country in the New Age Courses has increased 
from 8,611 in session 2022-23 to 42,338 in session 2025-26. The list of these 31 New 
Age Courses is at Annexure-II. 
 (c)  Release of funds under PM- SETU for Upgradation of ITIs  is contingent 
upon the formation of SPV. MSDE is carryi ng out necessary consultations to support 
and handhold States/UTs across various as pects of scheme roll-out. At present, 
proposals regarding upgradation of ITIs is yet to be received from State Steering 
Committee for consideration or approval of National Steering Committee (NSC). 
It is pertinent to mention that the Mi nistry has made significant progress in 
enabling States/UTs to move forward. 35 States/UTs have identified clusters, 32 have 
56
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
formed State Steering Committees, 25 have created State Budget Heads, 19 have 
floated EOIs for AIPs, and 12 have floated RFPs for inviting interest of industry. 
 (d)   Mandatory industry linkages, includin g provisions such as On-the-Job 
Training (OJT), facilitate structured en gagement between ITIs and industry. This 
exposure enables trainees to acquire hands-on experience in real work environments, 
align their skills with industry requirem ents, and develop essential employability 
competencies such as communication, t eamwork, and work ethics. Such industry-
integrated training enhances the job-read iness of ITI graduates and improves their 
prospects for wage employment. Further, Under the PM-SETU scheme, industry-led 
Special Purpose Vehicles (SPVs) would l ead the upgradation and be empowered to 
propose necessary interventions relating to curriculum redesign, training delivery 
models, infrastructure upgradation and in dustry exposure, in alignment with local 
industry needs and the Scheme guidelines. 
 (e)  The Directorate General of Training (DGT) under the aegis of the 
Ministry of Skill Development and Entrepreneur ship (MSDE) is implementing the Dual 
System of Training (DST) in ITIs under the CTS to strengthen industry linkage in skill 
training. Under DST, a substantial portion of practical training is conducted at industry 
premises, enabling trainees to gain ha nds-on experience on modern tools, 
equipment and machinery, thereby enhanc ing their employability. This enables 
industries to train candidates according to  their skill requirements while providing 
trainees with hands-on industry experience aligned with market demand. 
With effect from the academic session 20 23–24, all new affili ations granted in 
the third shift in ITIs have been made mandatory under DST mode, thereby promoting 
the adoption of DST mode acr oss ITIs. Enrolment in ITIs under DST during the last 
two sessions was 33,566 in 2024–25 and 39,136 in 2025–26 
Further, the proportion of trainees en rolled under DST relative to the total 
number of trainees in an ITI has been incorporated as a pa rameter in the Data Driven 
Grading Methodology (DDGM) for grading of  ITIs, with higher enrolment under DST 
resulting in a higher score under the said parameter. 
In addition, the Strategic Advisory and Reforms Taskforce for Holistic ITI 
Transformation (SARTHI) has been cons tituted under the chairmanship of the 
Minister of State (Independent Charge), MSDE, to provide advisory inputs on 
curriculum development and standardization, trade testing and certification reforms, 
accreditation and regulatory norms for ITIs , strengthening of industry–institute 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
57

 
 
linkages, and facilitation of inter-minister ial and institutional coordination under  
the CTS.

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Now Q. No. 394. 
Child Care Institutions 
*394. DR. ASHOK KUMAR MITTAL : Will the Minister of WOMEN AND CHILD 
DEVELOPMENT be pleased to state: 
(a)   whether social audits have exposed poor living conditions and inadequate 
safety protocols within Child Care Institutions (CCIs) across the country; 
(b)   the reasons for challenges under Missio n Vatsalya in ensuring the mental 
health and nutritional support of vulnerable children; 
(c)   the details of the shortage of traine d counselors, child protection officers 
and functional juvenile justice boards; 
(d)   the measures to strictly monitor an d shut down unregistered private CCIs ; 
and 
(e)   the steps taken to increase funding fo r family-based foster care and to 
overhaul the institutional care ecosystem? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF WOMEN AND CHILD 
DEVELOPMENT (SHRIMATI SAVITRI THAKUR): (a) to (e) A statement is laid on the 
Table of the House. 
 
 
64
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Statement 
 (a) The Ministry of women and Child De velopment is the nodal Ministry for the 
administration of Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act, 2015 (JJ 
Act, 2015) which is the primary legislation for ensuring safety, security, dignity and 
well-being of children and is implemented by all the States an d UTs including the 
State of Uttarakhand. The Act creates statutory structures at the State and District 
levels which include State Child Protection Society (SCPS), State Adoption Resource 
Agency (SARA), Child Welfare Committees (CWCs), Juvenile Justice Boards 
(JJBs), District Child Protection Units (DCPUs ). It also provides for establishment of 
Child Care Institutions (CCIs). 
Under the JJ Act 2015 (Sections 27-30), the Child Welfare Committees 
(CWCs) have been empowered to take decisi ons with regard to the children in need 
of care and protection, keeping their best inte rest in mind. They are also mandated to 
monitor the function of the Child Care Inst itutions (CCIs). As per Section 106 of the 
JJ Act 2015, the primary responsibility for the implementation of the Act lies with 
the State Governments and Union Territory (UT) Administrations. At the national and 
state level, the JJ Act provides the Nati onal/State Commissions for Protection of 
Child Rights to monitor the implementation of the Act (Section 109). 
Inspection Committees are mandated under section 54 of JJ Act, 2015 to visit 
such facilities housing children. District Magist rate is the nodal authority in the district 
for children in need of  care and protection and to take action on the findings of the 
report submitted by the Inspection Committees. The Ministry regularly follows up with 
the State and Union Territory Governments and various advisories have been issued 
so as to ensure the effective implementation of Mission Vatsalya Scheme. 
The Ministry regularly follows up wi th the State and Union Territory 
Governments and various advisories have been  issued so as to ensure the effective 
implementation of Mission Vatsalya Scheme. 
 (b) and (c) The Ministry of Women and Child Development is implementing a 
Centrally Sponsored Scheme, namely ‘Mission Vatsalya’, through the State and UT 
Governments to deliver various services for Children in Need of Care and Protection 
(CNCP) as well as Children in Conflict wi th Law (CCL). These services include 
Institutional Care and Non-Institutional Care.  The Child Care Institutions (CCIs) 
established under the Mission Vatsalya sc heme support, inter-alia, age-appropriate 
education, access to vocational training, recreation, health care, counselling 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
65

 
 
etc. Under non-institutional care, support is provided to the children through 
Sponsorship, Foster Care, Adoption and After Care. 
As per Mission Vatsalya Guidelines, a CCI having capacity of 50 children can 
have Special Unit for 10 children with sp ecial needs. Similarl y, a CCI having the 
capacity of 25 children can have Special Unit for 5 children. 
The scheme provides for Special Educat ors/therapist and Nurse required for 
such children in CCIs. The Scheme also pr ovides the institutions, inter-alia, with 
specialized equipment and materials like Ps ychological test materials, training 
materials for speech and language, teachi ng materials, wheel chairs. CCIs under 
Mission Vatsalya also provide counselling and psychological support services as a 
standard component of care. Rehabilitation frameworks include individual care plans, 
health services, and emotional support mechanisms. 
The Ministry of Women and Child Deve lopment in collaboration with Lal 
Bahadur Shastri National Academy of Ad ministration (LBSNAA), Mussoorie has 
developed an online training module on Ju venile Justice (Care and Protection for 
Children) Act, 2015 for capacity building of  multiple stakeholders such as State 
Governments and UT Administrations, district  authorities and others. Various training 
and capacity building programmes have been conducted for child protection 
functionaries and other stakeholders across the States and UTs. 
 (d)    Section 41 of the JJ Act, 2015 mandat es every institution which is meant, 
either wholly or partially, for housing ch ildren in need of care and protection or 
children in conflict with law, to be register ed under the Act. The act further provides 
State Governments the power to grant and cancel registration of Child Care 
Institutions which provide rehabilitation an d reintegration services as specified in 
section 53 of the Act. 
 (e)   Mission Vatsalya promotes family ba sed non-institutional care of children 
in difficult circumstances based on the princi ple of institutionalization of children as a 
measure of last resort. Unde r non-institutional care, support is provided to the 
children through Sponsorship, Foster Care , Adoption and After Care. The scheme 
provides Rs.4000/- per month to children  eligible for non-institutional care 
(Sponsorship/ Foster Care/ Aftercare) on cost sharing basis. 
The Ministry has also issued Model Fost er Care Guidelines, 2024 to ensure 
clarity, efficiency and compliance amongst the stakeholders. 
66
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
To improve the institutional care system, the Government of India has 
supported in establishing the statutory and service delivery structures under Mission 
Vatsalya Scheme, at State and District level namely State Child Protection Society 
(SCPS), State Adoption Resource Agen cy (SARA), Child Welfare Committees 
(CWCs), Juvenile Justice Boards (JJBs), District Child Protection Units (DCPUs) 
and Child Care Institutions (CCIs). Furthe r, under the Juvenile  Justice Amendment 
Act, 2021, the District Magistrates have been made the focal point for the 
administration of JJ Act.

MR. CHAIRMAN : Now, further discussion on the motion for consideration of The

GOVERNMENT BILLS 
$The Central Armed Police Forces (General Administration) Bill, 2026  
SHRI SAKET GOKHALE (West Bengal) : Hon’ble Chairman, Sir, I want to start by 
presenting a very unique scenario. Imag ine you are a person who has worked 
dedicatedly for an organization for the last, say, 20 to 25 years of your life. You have 
given all your life to that organization. You have been dedicated to that organization 
and then suddenly somebody is brought from the outside and made your boss, and is 
sitting over your head! That happens in the Bharatiya Janata Party, Sir. 
But, now, that is also being done in  the Central Armed Police Forces through 
this Bill. Sir, the CAPF -- a lot of peop le in our country do not know this -- is 
considered to be the first line of defence in our country . The armed forces form the 
second line of defence. The CAPF forms the first line of defence. They are posted in 
deserts, in high altitudes, snow peaks an d in hostile terrains. There is a reason, 
colloquially, the CAPF forces are known as paramilitary forces. The reason for that is, 
because their job description is much closer  to what the armed forces do as opposed 
to what civilian police does. Therefore, we sh ould take it at face value that civilian 
policing is very different from the work th at the Central Armed Police forces do. When 
a CAPF officer writes the exam to join as a Group ‘A’ officer in any of these services, 
they do it specifically to work in the param ilitary. Similarly, when  a person writes the 
UPSC exam and becomes an IPS officer, th ey are doing it because they specifically 
want to work in civilian policing. These tw o aspects are very diff erent. Just because 
they call it the Central Armed Police Forces  doesn't mean they function and do the 
duties that a regular civilian police force does. Sir, when we look at this Bill and when 
the top positions are now being allocated to  IPS officers in th e CAPF, the question 
that we should be asking is: In the history of India, has a CAPF officer of the rank, say 
a Deputy Commandant, ever been appointed as a Superi ntendent of Police in a 
district? No, Sir; it has never happened. Ar e CAPF officers sent  on deputation to 
agencies like the CBI or the other agencies ? No, they are not. Then, why is this 
                   
$  Further consideration continued on a motion moved on the 25th March, 2026.   
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
69

 
 
discrimination being shown in one direction only? We will have IPS officers exclusively 
leading forces, whereas CAPF officers are no t going to get access and promotions to 
get to that level in their own forces. Sir,  we encounter CAPF personnel on a day-to-
day basis. If you go to airports, there is CI SF. This Parliament is protected by CISF, 
which is also a Central Armed Police Force. We should, sometimes, see the working 
conditions of CAPF personnel deployed in different places. I remember, when we 
were children, 1993 Bombay riots had happ ened. Rapid Action Force, which is a 
battalion of the CRPF, was deployed at the area where we were living. They used to 
play cricket with us. They had no gifts to give us because they really had nothing to 
give. They were living in those temporary ba rracks. They used to give us soap cases 
because that is the only thing that they had. Sir, they serve in the most hostile places 
for about 10 to 11 months a year. When they serve on the front, it is already difficult. 
But, even in civilian postings, when they ar e sent for riot control or maintaining law 
and order situations, they stay in temporar y barracks. The facilities that are provided 
to the CAPF personnel are not great. What is ironic is that the CAPF does pretty much 
similar job as to what the armed forces do . But, sadly, CAPF have never been given 
even a fraction of the facilities that are extended to other forces . Today, the life of an 
IPS officer is far more comfor table than the life of an equivalent rank officer in the 
CAPF, be it a Deputy Commandant or Co mmandant. The sad part is: To become a 
commandant in the CAPF, you do about 15 to  18 years of service. Then, you get to 
rank of Commandant, which is equivalent to a Superintendent of Police in the IPS. In 
those 15 to 18 years, an IPS officer becomes an IG. We  are seeing this very unique 
situation, in the CAPF, where a CAPF offi cer joined in 1987, has a boss who has 
joined in 1997. This is the most humiliati ng experience for anyone in uniform, where 
someone 10 years junior to you is coming and becoming your boss. And, with this 
legislation, you are giving the legal right to them to be their boss and saying you are 
only going to get up to this level. Beyond th at level, it is going to be an IPS officer 
only. 
Sir, why are we systematically destroyi ng the structure of our forces? With the 
armed forces, you scrapped in recruitment;  you brought in the Agniveer Scheme. 
With CAPF, you are bringing in this Bill. Today, what is the motivation for a young 
person to join the Central Armed Police Fo rces? I will give you a simple statistics, 
which I think may even open the eyes of th e Government to this draconian legislation 
that they are bringing. Sir, in the last 20 years, 1,100 Gazetted officers of the CAPF -- 
I am talking about Assistant Commandant , Deputy Commandant, Commandant rank 
officers --died in the line of duty. In the same 20 years, about 30 to 40 IPS officers 
70
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
were killed in the line of duty. With this Bill, you are telling the young people who want 
to join the CAPF that you join as Group ‘A’ officer, we want you to go and take the 
bullets on the border, but, when it comes to  leading your own force, we do not trust 
you. We are going to bring an IPS officer fr om Odisha Cadre or somewhere else to sit 
on your head to lead your force. How fair is that, Sir? You want them to go and take 
bullets. But, you are not going to give them  the right to lead their own force where 
they have served for 20 plus years! It is extremely unfortunate. 
I will tell you the reason why this is be ing done. It is nothing but a series of 
politicization of different instit utions. But, I will tell you what  is going on at the ground 
level. Sir, I come from a police family. We pretty much understand these dynamics. 
We are seeing them work out first hand. CAPF Group ‘A’ officers, be it Assistant 
Commandant, Deputy Commandant or Command ant, they are serving on the front. 
They are doing front posting. Also, there is no concept of rest posting in CAPF. So, a 
CISF officer on a front posting will be gu arding a dam on the border. In a ‘rest 
posting’, they will be guarding an airport in the city. There is nothing called a ‘rest 
posting’ for them. Sir, the thing is that the CAPF officers do not ordinarily come into 
contact with politicians. कोई  काम ही नहीं होता है, कोई लेना-देना ही नहीं होता है। No one 
is picking up the phone and as king them to do this or do  that. On the other hand, by 
virtue of their jobs, IPS officers are requ ired to maintain regular contact with the 
political leadership. So what happens? A CAPF  officer will never work in the interest 
of a certain politician or a political party. A CAPF officer today is posted in Punjab. 
Tomorrow, he or she might be posted in Jammu Kashmir. Then, he or she might be 
posted in Assam. So, there is no concept of a ‘ruling party’ for them. It changes 
depending on where they ar e going and the areas where they serve. There is no 
concept of a ruling party or an opposition pa rty. You want to bring in the IPS to head 
these forces because you want political control over these forces. It is a very 
dangerous trend. There are certain institut ions today that we are very proud of. 
Despite a lot of disagreements we might ha ve with the Treasury Benches or with the 
Government, we are proud of the fact that  certain institutions — particularly the 
armed forces — are above politics today. And, that is the reason our country, despite 
so many challenges on so many fronts, has managed to not just survive but thrive as 
a democracy. So, the moment you bring politics into matters of national security, it is 
very dangerous. And, today, that is exactly what is being done with the CAPF. It is 
incomprehensible as to what  the purpose behind this B ill is. One thing that we can 
prima facie see is that this is turning into a jo b-creation scheme. I will tell you why.  
There are several retired IPS officers here  in this House as well who are hon’ble 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
71

 
 
Members. About 25-30 years ago, the head of  a State police used to be an IGP-rank 
officer. आईजीपी से ऊपर डीजीपी होता था।  आईजीपी रȅक होता था। But, today, we have at 
least six to seven officers of DGP rank with in a certain State. Then, you have created 
the post of Additional DG. Now, there is a Director General of Police in charge of the 
State police, plus you have postings like DG (Housing), DG (Home Guards), DG 
(Fire Services), etc. Why is this being done? In the Army, it is very different. You get 
up to a Colonel and then the pyramid starts  narrowing. You either retire as a Colonel 
or, if you are really meritorious, you rise to  the rank of a General. That is not the case 
with the police forces. In the police for ces, you want to accommodate as many IPS 
officers as you can at the top. So, no w, the Central Armed Police Forces have 
become a place to park IPS officers. I will  tell you how politicisation works. I am 
seeing this in my State happening right now. In 2021, elections were held in eight 
phases in Bengal. There were 1,071 compan ies of CAPF that were deployed — eight 
phases, 1,071 companies. Fine! It was a long  election. Now, there is an election in 
twophases and the deployment has been incr eased from 1,071 to 2,400 companies.  
Sir, I wish the Government would have brou ght in a Bill to care about the welfare of 
CAPF personnel. Today, we are going to have these 2,400 companies in Bengal. 
There is an LPG shortage. It is now th e State Government's concern how we are 
going to feed all these officers and ensure their welfare when there is a massive 
shortage of LPG in the market. The Union Go vernment doesn't care. For them, it is a 
political agenda — just deploy the Central Forces and let the West Bengal 
Government handle it. I only wish that instead of bringing Bills like these, the 
Government would bring in a Bill to ensure more facilities and better welfare for CAPF 
officers. 
Today let me talk about the ground rea lity. This is not about politics. See 
whether the children of IPS officers go to school comfortably, and then see whether 
the children of CAPF officers  go to school. Why have we created this dual system? 
These are the officers who give their lives for the country. Why have we created such 
a system where they don't get respect ou tside, among the public, because they 
never interact with the public, and you are not giving them respect within their own 
force as well? They work with their men and women for 20 years but, suddenly, you 
are telling them they are not fit to lead this force. Only an IPS officer who will come for 
5 years — they are going to spend 3 years just learning about the force -- how are 
they going to lead that armed police force? Sir, this Bill has been brought, -- I don't 
know for what malicious reason -- but I humbly request that this Bill should be 
withdrawn. The Government should think more about the we lfare of the CAPF, rather

†ﺮѧѧѧیﺷ ﻼﻡѧﻏ یﻋﻞ : ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ںیﻡ ﻩیﻭﺵ ﺮѧѧѧѧﭘ یﻩ ﺎﺕѧѧѧѧﺑ ﺮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺭﮨﺎ ﻮںѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺎﺏѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﻨﺠ ںیﻡ ﺰﺍﺭﻭںѧѧﮨ ںیﺟﺎﻥ ﺊѧѧѧѧ۔ںیﮔ 
ﺎیﺍѧѧѧﺳ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧﮨﻮﺗ ﮯѧѧﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ یﮨﻤﺎﺭ ﺎﺭڈﺭѧѧѧѧﺑ ﻮﺭٹیﺱѧѧѧѧیﮐ ﻮﺭﺱѧѧѧѧѧﻓ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﭧѧѧѧٹیﺍﺳ یک ﻮﻟﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘ ﭧѧѧѧٹیﺍﺳ ﭧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮔﻮﺭﻧﻤﻨ

ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﻮگѧѧی۔ﮨ ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ یﺏ ﺱیﺍ ﻑیﺍ ﮯѧѧ ѧﺳ ﭻѧѧѧѧѧﺑ ﺮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮﺉѧѧѧѧیﮐ infiltrator ﺪﺭѧѧѧﺍﻧ ﺁ ،ﺍیگ ﻮѧѧѧѧﺗ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻮﮐ ﻮﻝѧѧѧѧﺱیﭘ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ 
ﺍَﺳﻴﺴﭧ ﻮﻥѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯѧѧﮨ – یﺱ  ﺍے یپ ﻑیﺍ ,ﻦیﻝѧѧѧѧﮐ یﺱ ﺍے یپ ﻑیﺍ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻮﮐ ﻮﻝѧѧѧѧﺱیﭘ ﺍﻭﺭ یﺏ ﺱیﺍ 
ﻑیﺍ ںیﻡ ﺟﻮ ﻢѧѧѧѧیﻭﻥیﮐ ﻦیکѧѧѧﺷ پیگ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﺟﻮ ﺮѧѧѧѧﻦیڈﺍﺋѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺸ پیگ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﺟﻮ ﺫﻣہ یﺩﺍﺭ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ پیگ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ 
ﮑﻮѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ ﻩی 2026 ﺎѧѧѧ ѧﮐ ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ ﻮﺭﺍѧѧѧѧﭘ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ

ﻩی' ﺪﺭیکѧѧѧیﻧ ﺘﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳﺸﺴ ﻮﻟﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘ ﺑِﻞ( ﺎﻣﺎﻥѧѧﻩیﺳ ﻦѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﺮﺷﺎﺳ )،کیﻭﺩﻫ 2026' ﮑﻮѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ ﺒﻮﻁѧѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻀ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯѧѧﮨ

ѧ،ﮔ یﺍﺱ ﻮﻻﺝیٹѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﮐﻨ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﺗﻬѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﻭﻣﻦیﻩ ﻞیﺍѧѧѧѧѧﻧﮕ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﺗﻬѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﻮﺁﺭڈﻥѧѧѧѧﻦیﮐѧѧѧﺷ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﺗﻬѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺳ people-

ﭩﺮﮐﭽﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺍﻧﻔﺮﺍﺳ ﮑﻮﻝѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ یﺁﺩ ﺎﺭѧѧѧیﺳ ﻮﺩﻫﺎﺅںѧѧѧﺳ یک ﺮﻭﻋﺎﺕѧѧѧﺷ ﺪﺑﻬﺎﻭﻧﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﭗѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭨﺎﺋ یک ﻮﺭﺱѧѧѧѧѧﺯیﻓ یک

ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ یﺱ ﺍے یپ ﻑیﺍ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻮﮐ ﻮﻟﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﺗﻬѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﭼﺎﺭ ﻬѧѧѧﻩیﻣﮑ ﺯﻭںیچ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺖیﺩѧѧѧѧیﮐﻬ ۔ںیﻩ ﺭﺭﺯﻡیٹ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ 
ﺖیﺩѧѧѧѧیﮐﻬ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧﺁﭘﻨ ﺎیﺩѧѧѧѧﮐﻬ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻢѧѧѧѧѧѧﺭیﮐﺸ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺎﺏѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﻨﺠ ںیﻡ ﺭﺭﺯﻡیٹ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ 40 ﺰﺍﺭѧѧﮨ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﺍﺩﻩیﺯ ںیﺟﺎﻥ 
ﺊѧѧѧѧ۔ںیﮔ ﮑﻮѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ ﻮﻥѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺱیڈﺭیﺍ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯ؟ѧѧﮨ ﻼﺋﭧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧﻧﮑﺴ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺭِﺯﻟﭧ  ﮨﻤﺎﺭے ﺎﻣﻨﮯѧѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺮѧѧѧѧﺍﮔ ﻢѧѧﮨ ﺎﺭڈﺭѧѧѧѧﺑ 
ﻮﺭٹیﺱѧѧѧѧیﮐ ﻮѧѧѧ ѧﮐ یﮨﺎﺉ کیٹ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﺮѧѧѧѧﮯیﮐѧѧѧѧѧ،ﻧﮕ ﻮѧѧѧѧﺗ ﻭﻩ یﮨﺎﺉ ﺑِﺮڈ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﮯیکѧѧѧﺳ ﮍѧѧѧﺎ؟یﻟѧѧѧѧﮔ ﮑﮯѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ ےیﻝ 
ﻮﺉѧѧѧѧیﮐ ﺎﻧﻮﻥѧѧѧѧѧﻗ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﺎﺉѧѧѧѧѧѧںیﺑﻨ ﮯѧѧѧѧ،ﮔ ﻮѧѧѧѧﺗ ﺍیک ﻭﻩ ﺮﮐﻮﻟﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧﺳ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﺎ؟یﭼﻞѧѧѧѧﮔ ﺍیک ﻭﻩ یﮨﻤﺎﺭ یﻣﺮﺽ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ 
ﺎ؟یﭼﻞѧѧѧѧﮔ 
ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ یﺱ ﺍے یپ ﻑیﺍ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺖѧѧѧѧѧﺑﮩ ﺎﺭٹѧѧѧѧﭧیﺍﻣﭙѧѧѧﻧ ﺎﻡѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﻩی ﮯѧѧѧѧѧﺩﻧﮕ ﺎﺩѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻓﺴ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﭩﺮﻭﻝѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻨ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺮѧѧѧѧﺍﮔ 
ﻢѧ
ѧﮨ ﭩﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﮨﺴ ںیﻡ ﻬیﺩѧѧѧѧ،ںیﮐ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮔﻮﮔ ںیﻡ ﺮچѧѧѧﺳ ﺮѧѧѧѧ،ںیﮐ ﻮѧѧѧѧﺗ ﻢѧѧںیﮨ ہѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﺘ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭼﻠﺘ ﮯѧѧﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺮѧѧѧѧﺍﮔ ﮯیڈѧѧѧﻟ ہѧѧѧﻧ 
ﺎѧѧѧѧѧ،ﮨﻮﺗ ﻮѧѧѧѧﺗ 1969 ںیﻡ ﺪﺁﺑﺎﺩﺍﺡѧѧѧѧﻣ ںیﻡ ﺟﻮ riots ﻮﺋﮯѧѧѧѧѧﮨ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺗﻬ ﻭﻩ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﻮﺗﮯѧѧѧѧѧ۔ﮨ یﺩﻝ ںیﻡ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺪﺭﺍѧѧѧﺍﻧ یﺝ 
یک ﺮﮐﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧ،یﺗ ﻭﮨﺎں ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺎﻧﮕﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺱیﮐ یک ﺮﮐﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺗ ﺍﻭﺭ ﻢѧѧѧѧیﻭﻥیﮐ ﻦیکѧѧѧﺷ پیگ یک ﻭﺟہ 
ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻞѧѧѧѧﺲیﻭﺍﺋѧѧѧﻧ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﭙﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧڈیﺍﺳ  ﻮﻧﮯѧѧѧѧﮨ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻊѧѧѧﻣﻮﻗ ۔ﻣﻼ یﺱ ﺍے یپ ﻑیﺍ ﺍٓﻓﻴﺴﺮ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻮﮐ ﻮﻟﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ 
ﮉѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻤﺎﻧ یﻥیﻝ ﻮﺕѧѧیﮨ ﮯѧѧﮨ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺟﺐ ﮏѧѧѧﺗ ﺎﺉѧѧѧڈیڈﺳ ۔۔۔ﺍیک ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺩﻭ ﻢѧѧѧѧﭧیﮐѧѧѧﺯیﻭﻧ ںیﻩ - ﮍﺍѧѧѧѧﺑ ﻤﺎﺝѧѧ،ﺳ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧﭼﻬﻮﭨ 
ﻤﺎﺝѧѧ۔ﺳ ﮍےѧѧѧѧﮔﻬ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺲѧѧѧѧﺁﭘ ںیﻡ ﮍﺗﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﻬ ،ںیﻩ ﻦیﻝѧѧѧѧﮐ یﺱ ﺍے یپ ﻑیﺍ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺪѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻘﺼ ﺍیک ﮯ؟ѧѧﮨ ﮑﺎѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ 
ﺪѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻘﺼ riots ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﭩﺮﻭﻝѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧﮐﻨ  ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﻧ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﮑﮯѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ ےیﻝ pro-active policy ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﻨﺎﻧ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ یﺭ ﭧیﺍѧѧѧѧﻭیﮐ 
ﺎﻝѧѧѧѧیﺱیﭘ ﺟﮩﺎں ﺍﺱ ںیﻡ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻢѧѧﮨ political nurturing ﺮﺗﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐ ۔ںیﻩ 
ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺍٓﺳﺎﻡ ںیﻡ AASU ﮯѧѧѧﻧ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻮﮐ ﻮںѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻮﮔ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮﻕѧѧѧﺣﻘ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ کیﺍ ﭧیﻣﻮﻭﻡѧѧѧﻧ ﺮﻭﻉѧѧѧﺷ ﺍیک ﺎѧѧѧѧ،ﺗﻬ ﺟﻮ 
deadliest riots ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺭﻭپ ںیﻡ یﺟﺎﻥ یﺟﺎﺕ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺍﺱ ﺖѧѧѧﻭﻗ ﺎںѧﻭﮨ ﺮﮐﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﺲѧѧѧѧﮐ یک ﻬѧѧѧѧѧ؟یﺗ یﺩﻝ ںیﻡ 
ﺪﺭﺍѧѧ
ѧﺍﻧ یﺝ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧںیﺗ ﺍﻭﺭ ﻭﮨﺎں ﺎﻧﮕﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺱیﮐ یک ﺮﮐﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧی۔ﺗ ﻩی ﻮﻥѧѧѧѧﮐ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧﺟﺎﻧﺘ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺁپ ﻬیﺩѧѧѧѧﮐ 
،ےیﺟﺊیﻝ ﻣﺮﺍﺩﺍٓﺑﺎﺩ ںیﻡ ﺟﻮ ﻮﺍѧѧ،ﮨ ﺍﺱ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻢѧѧﮨ ﭻѧѧѧѧѧﺑ ﮑﺘﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧ۔ﺗﻬ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﭽﻬﻠ 14 ﺎﻟﻮںѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻦѧѧѧیﺳ ﻋہیﺵ 
ﺎﺩѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻓﺴ ﻭںیک ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﻮѧѧﮨ ﮯѧѧﺭﮨ ں؟یﻩ ﻠﺌﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﻮѧѧﮨ ﮯѧѧﺭﮨ ،ںیﻩ ہیکѧѧѧѧѧﻭﻧﮑ ﺁﺝ ﻮﻡѧѧﮨ ﭩﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻨﺴ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ pro-
active role ﮯѧ ѧ،ﮨ reactive role ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﻢѧѧںیﮨ political nurturing ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﺮﻥѧѧѧѧیﮐ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﮨﻤﺎﺭے ﺮѧѧѧѧﺍﻭﭘ 
ﺟﻮ ﮍےѧѧѧѧﺑ ﮍےѧѧѧѧﺑ ﮯѧѧѧѧﺩﻫﺒ ںیﻩ - ﺎﮔﻠﭙﻮﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﻬ riots ںیﻡ  ﺰﺍѧѧﺭﻭںﮨ ﻮگѧѧѧﻟ ﻣﺎﺭے ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﮔﺌ ﺎﻣﺒﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑ riots ںیﻡ  
ﺰﺍﺭﻭںѧѧﮨ ﻮگѧѧѧﻟ ﻣﺎﺭے ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ۔ﮔﺌ ﻮںѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻮﮔ ﮯѧѧѧﻧ ﻭﻭٹ ﺍیﺩ ﮯѧѧﮨ ﺍﻭﺭ ﻭﻭٹ ﺮیﺩѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺍﺩﻫﺮ 35-35 ﻡیﺍ  ﺯیپ ﺎﺋﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﻨ 
۔ںیﻩ ﻞѧѧѧѧﺵیﺍﮐﻬ یﺝ ﻑیچ ﭩﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻨﺴ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺗﻬ یﺩﻝ ںیﻡ ﺎﻧﮕﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺱیﮐ یک ﺮﮐﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧی۔ﺗ ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺮﻭѧѧѧ ѧﭘ  ﻮیﺍѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﭩ 
ﺭﻭﻝ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﺍﻥ ﺯﻭںیچ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﺎѧѧѧѧﺑﭽ ﺟﺎ ﮑﺘﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﻢѧѧﮨ ﺍﻥ ﺖѧѧѧѧѧﺑﮩ ﺯﻭںیچ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﭻѧѧѧѧѧﺑ ﮑﺘﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧ۔ﺗﻬ

ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ںیﻡ ﮑﺎѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ gist ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﺘﺎﻧ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧﭼﺎﮨﺘ ﻮںѧѧ۔،ﮨ ﮑﺎѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ nutshell ﻩی  ﮯѧѧﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﭙﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻡیﺳ ﻮﺭٹѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺮیﻝѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺖѧѧѧѧѧﺑﮩ 
ﻮﻻѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑ ﺍیگ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻩی ﭙﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧﻡیﺳ ﻮﺭٹѧѧѧѧﮐ یک ﺎﺉѧѧѧѧڈیﮔ ﻦѧѧѧѧѧﻻﺋ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻦѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻟﻨﮕﻬ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﻦیﻝѧѧѧѧﮐ ںیﻡ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﺘﺎﻧ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧﭼﺎﮨﺘ 
ﻮںѧѧ،ﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻩی کیﻭﺩﻫ ﭙﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻡیﺳ ﻮﺭٹѧѧѧѧﮐ یک ﮉѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮔﺎﺋ ﻦѧѧѧѧѧﻻﺋ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮉﻭﺭﺱیﺍѧѧѧﻧ ﺮﺗﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮﺋﮯѧѧѧѧѧ،ﮨ security ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ  
ﺱیڈﺭیﺍ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﻩی ﺮﻭﭨﻢیﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧﭧیﮐѧѧѧﻧ  ںیﻡ ﭧیﻭﻥیѧѧѧѧیﻓﺎﺭﻣ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧﻻﺗ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﺮﻭﺱѧѧѧﺳ ﮉѧѧѧѧѧѧﻨﺲیﮐﻨѧѧѧѧѧﺷ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ 
ﺱیڈﺭیﺍ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯѧѧﮨ ﺍﻭﺭ ﮏѧѧیﺍﻧ ﺯیﻣﻞیﻑ یک ﻮѧѧѧﺩﻫﺎﺅںیﺳ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺱیڈﺭیﺍ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ یﺱ ﺍے یپ 
ﻑیﺍ ںیﻡ ﺟﻮ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﭩﺮﻧ ﻨﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﺮﻭﻣﻮﺷ ،ںیﻩ ﻮѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﮑ ﻩی کیﻭﺩﻫ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﭽﺎﺗ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ The Bill mandates fixed 
deputation quotas for IPS Officers in se nior role with 50 per cent of Inspector 
Generals.  ﺎﺱѧѧѧѧﭘﭽ ﺪیﻑѧѧﺻ یﺱ ﺍے یپ ﻑیﺍ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ ﭻѧѧѧѧѧﺑ ۔ﺍیگ 
Then, 67 per cent of Additional Director General posts; but this is to ensure 
experienced leadership. This Bill ensures co ntinuity by retaining existing recruitment 
rules until formally revised, protects all financial benefits of Group ‘A’ officers while not 
imposing any financial burden on the public exchequer. 
†ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ںیﻡ ﺍٓﺧﺮ ںیﻡ ﻩی ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﮩﻮﻧﮕ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻢѧѧںیﮨ ﺍﺱ ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﭙﻮﺭٹѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﻧ ﮯیﭼﺎﻩѧѧѧѧ۔ﺋ ﻩی ﭧѧѧѧﺮیﺍﻧѧѧѧѧﻦیﮔѧѧѧﺷ 
ﺎѧѧѧѧѧﻻﺗ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﻩی ﭙﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻡیﺳ ﻮﺭٹѧѧѧѧﮐ یک ﮓѧѧѧѧﺭﻭﻟﻨ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ endorse  ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺮﺩﺍﺭѧѧѧﺳ ﭧѧѧѧѧﻝیﭘ یﺝ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮﺱѧѧѧѧѧﺯیﻓ 
ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ integrate  ﺮﻧﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺟﻮ doctrine ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﮑﻮѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ ﻩی endorse ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ںیﻡ ﺎﻧﮕﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺱیﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ 
ڈﺭﺱیﻝ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﮩﻮﻧﮕ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺮﺩﺍﺭѧѧѧﺳ ﭧѧѧѧѧﻝیﭘ یﺝ ﺎѧѧѧ ѧﮐ  ﺟﻮ ﭧѧѧѧﺮیﺍﻧѧѧѧѧﻦیﮔѧѧѧﺷ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺭﻭﻝ ﺎѧѧѧѧ،ﺗﻬ ﮑﻮѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ 
ﭙﻮﺭٹѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﺮѧѧѧѧ۔ںیﮐ ﮑﺮѧѧѧѧѧ۔ﻩیﺷ ﺟﮯ ﺪѧѧѧѧﮨﻨ !
MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN: Now, Dr. Fauzia Khan. 
DR. FAUZIA KHAN (Maharashtra) : Sir, I rise to oppose this Bill. I wish to speak as a 
voice for thousands of officers who protect our borders and fight in the jungles -- the 
BSF, the CRPF, the CISF, the ITBP and the SSB -- each with its own terrain, its own 
culture, its own institutional memory built over decades. The Government is using the 
Parliament to override a fina l binding Supreme Court verdict. This is not legislation. 
This is judicial evasion wearing the clothes of law. Imagine joining one of these forces 
as an Assistant Commandant, young, patr iotic and ready to serve. Sixteen years 
have passed. You have fought insurgents. You have earned your promotion and yet 
you have not received it. In Gadchiroli, in Maharashtra, our CRPF officers are those in 
the forest right now fighting naxalism. Just last month, the Union Home Minister 
chaired a meeting with Maharashtra, Chha ttisgarh and other States to intensify 
operations. The Government proudly declares that India will be naxal free by 31 st of 
March. I ask: Who will do that work? The IPS officer on deputation or the CAPF 
officer who has spent years on the ground? You want a naxal free India on paper.  
These officers want it on gr ound. They, at least, deserve a career that respects that 
sacrifice. This Bill claims to bring legislativ e clarity. This Bill claims to bring legislative

clarity but what it actually does is impose  one single Central law over forces. This

SHRI MANAN KUMAR MISHRA (Bihar): Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, I rise to support the 
Central Armed Police Forces Bill, 2026, a tr ansformative and structurally necessary 
reform that seeks to rationalise, mo dernise and humanise the administrative 
94
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
framework governing our CAPFs. Under Articl e 355 of the Constitution of India, the 
Union has a solemn duty to protect ever y State against external aggression and 
internal disturbances. The Central Armed Po lice Forces are the principal instruments 
through which this constitutional obligation is discharged. Sir, in Service, prudence, 
consistency and predictability are the corner stones of fairness. The hon’ble Supreme 
Court has repeatedly emphasised  that arbitrariness in administrative action violates 
Article 14. This Bill, therefore, brings codification, uniformity and legal certainty, 
ensuring that the governance of CAPFs is no longer dependent on discretion but 
guided by statute. 
Sir, at present, the five major CAPFs operate under separate statutes, leading 
to fragmented service conditions and freque nt litigations. The Bill addresses this by 
creating a single, unified statutory framework for recruitment, promotion and 
deputation. This Bill formalises and stre ngthens the long-established practice of 
appointing IPS officers to senior leadership positions with in CAPFs, thereby ensuring 
continuity, stability and administrative cohe rence. By statutorily providing for the 
deputation of IPS officers, the Bill secure s a consistent and me rit-based leadership 
pipeline at the highest level. This framew ork draws upon the extensive, pan-India 
policing experience of IPS officers, enabling them to effectively navigate complex law 
and order situations, counter-insurgency operations and inter-jurisdictional 
challenges. The Bill addresses the long-sta nding issue of career stagnation among 
approximately 13,000 Group ‘ А’ CAPF cadre officers by creating additional senior 
posts through focussed structural reforms. It helps ease persistent promotional 
bottlenecks and acknowledges the experience of in-house officers. 
Sir, for years, the welfare, housing, and healthcare needs of CAPF personnel 
remained inadequately addressed, creating gaps in support for both personnel and 
their families. The Bill will not affect the status quo and ensures continuity of all service 
benefits, allowances, pensions, and welfar e measures. Under the Modi Government, 
between 2019 and 2025, over 16,000 housin g units have been constructed improving 
the housing satisfaction from a mere 33 per cent in 2014 to a targeted 74 per cent in 
2024. 
सर, आज देश मȂ नक्सलवाद पूरी तरह समाÃत हो गया है। यह हमारे दूरदशीर् गृह मंतर्ी, 
अिमत शाह जी की सफल नीितयȗ और दृढ़ संकÊप का पिरणाम है। Under the UPA
 
Government, the absence of a unified co mmand contributed to serious setbacks, 
including the 2010 Dantewada attack th at killed 76 CRPF personnel. Modi 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
95

 
 
Government has strengthened the IPS-le d CAPF command, enabling coordinated, 
intelligence-based operations like Operation Kagaar and Operation Black Forest. 
Sir, reversing years of the UPA Govern ment’s neglect, the Modi Government 
has decisively accelerated force modern ization through Modernisation Plan-IV, 
backed by a dedicated financial outlay of Rs.1,523 crore. This comprehensive 
upgrade seamlessly integrates drones, advanced communication systems, protective 
gear, surveillance networks, and armour ed vehicles into daily operations. 
Furthermore, border infrastructure has been  aggressively fortified with 591 Km of 
fencing and 1,159 Km of floodlights along the Bangladesh border. 
Sir, championing women's empowermen t in national security, the Modi 
Government also approved the establishment of the first all women battalion for the 
CISF. (Time-bell rings)

approximately 8,659 cases admitted by the NCLT, 3,865 cases have been resolved.

Now, I come to average haircut. Haircut has been mentioned.  Creditors have 
faced an average haircut of approximately 67 per cent to 70 per cent on their admitted 
claims. This means creditors, including banks, typically recover only 30 per cent to 33 
per cent of the debt owed to them. आदरणीय राधा मोहन जी बोल रहे थे िक एनपीए कैसे

83 per cent. This is the scenario. Now, rega rding precise real-time total, specifically, 
under nationalised banks’ heading, estima tes suggest that the nationalised banks 
overall have foregone roughly Rs.8 lakh cr ore through haircuts alone since the IBC’s 
inception. Therefore, you can understand how the NPA has been reduced and banks 
are making profit, as it is claimed. Though it is rightly claimed, but it is because of 
this. Then, across all successful resolution plans, -- forget about the banks, I am 
talking about successful resolutions in enti rety -- till late 2025,  creditors realised 
approximately Rs.3.99 trillion against admitted claims worth roughly Rs.12.31 trillion.  
So, the amount of admitted claims was Rs.1 2.31 trillion, and the realised amount was 
only Rs.3.99 trillion. This is the failure of pu rpose of provision of haircut. This Bill fails 
to curb massive haircuts for creditors, which is a tool to reap assets rather than truly 
dissolve them. Other than this, there are infrastructure bottlenecks.  Strict timelines, 
like 40-day admission, are unachievable, as  NCLT and NCLAT are severely hindered 
by vacancies and backlogs, making the Amendments impractical. I am not going 
Clause-by-Clause because of the paucity of time. Th e new Creditor-Initiated 
Insolvency Resolution Process (CIIRP) allows selected financial institutions to initiate 
insolvency without judicial oversight, whic h could be misused, will lead to litigation 
and trigger insolvencies prematurely based sole ly on default rather than viability.  This 
violates Article 14 of the Constitution in as much as they had made a distinction 
between two sets of claimants. 
The Bill grants the Central Government  sweeping powers to frame rules for 
complex areas like cross-border insolvency and group insolvency.  This constitutes 
excessive delegation of essential legislativ e functions to the Executive branch, which 
is absolutely unconstitutional, as it lacks clear guiding principles  within the primary 
law.  This is a point of law that I am raising before this august House.  Then, the Bill 
allows these Executive rules to modify or ad opt existing provisions of the IBC or the

Companies Act, which bypasses parliamentary scrutiny of major policy shifts.  This is

comprehensive legislation be brought, removing the contentious sections as 
mentioned above, and to revive the claims  of the genuine claimants in a manner so 
that they are not subjected to so much of haircuts. 
Finally, this Bill also leaves the Govern ment as unsecured creditor. Previously, 
the Government was the secured creditor. In all the claim matters in this country, we 
have always seen that the Government, th e Government organizations or the public 
sector undertakings are mostly the secured creditors. But, now, after this 
amendment, Government will be unsecured cr editor.  What if the Government money

SHRIMATI RAJATHI (Tamil Nadu) : Sir, this IBC (Amendment) Bill delegates four 
legislative functions of this Parliament and gives away that responsibility to the 
Executive. Clause 59A of the Amendment Bill empowers the Union Government to 
prescribe the manner and conditions for co nducting insolvency proceedings in case 
of group insolvency, where two or more co rporate debtors form a group. I would like 
to clearly point out another provisio n where the amendment outsourced our 
Parliament's function to the Executive. Clause 67 of the Amendment Bill empowers 
the Union Government to prescribe Rules relating to cross-border insolvency 
proceedings. Now, every aspect of the cross-border insolvency is left to the control 
of the Executive. It is a core legislative f unction of the Parliament that should not be 
given away. We should also note that the 2018 Report of the Insolvency Law 
Committee led by Shri Injeti Srinivas, the th en Secretary of the Ministry of Corporate 
Affairs, recommended incorporating the cross border insolvency framework as a 
chapter into the Insolvency Code.  It is unf ortunate that the Government is sidelining 
the role of the Parliament. 
During the deliberations of the Sel ect Committee, representatives of the 
National Company Law Tribunal informed that the NCLT was established initially 
under the Companies Act but after the IBC introduction, additional responsibility 
under the Insolvency Code also was placed  on the NCLT. This was done without 
increasing the number of member s. They also highlighted infrastructure challenges 
with NCLT functioning in old buildings and lack of permanent employees with around 
90 per cent staff working as contract staff. We should focus on having more number 
of members, Benches, more permanent staf f and infrastructure. I urge the hon’ble 
Minister to assure the House that the Gove rnment is taking measures to strengthen 
the capacity of overburden ed and understaffed NCLT in handling additional 
responsibilities put on them on a regular basis. Without addressing these structural 
problems, adding an additional responsib ility on NCLT will further increase the delay

admitted claims. This is also one major pr oblem, which needs to be addressed.  
Under the current Section 53 waterfal l mechanism, MSMEs and other small 
operational creditors receive negligible recoveries because amounts are exhausted by 
secured financial creditors. Sir,  operational creditors lack voting rights in the 
Committee of Creditors, leaving them with no influence or resolution plans. This 
needs to be addressed. 
The CoC often lacks a formal code of conduct, leading to delayed decisions on 
interim finances. Also, India has lacked a robust statutory framework for substantial 
consolidation of coordination with foreign jurisdictions, leaving it to fragmented 
proceedings and value erosion. Also, the new creditor initiated insolvency resolution 
process is currently restricted to specified financial institutions only. That also needs 
to be addressed. These are all the issues. 
Now, I would like to make some suggest ions also. We need to implement a 
formal index to measure the economic cost of adjudicatory lag to create institutional 
accountability. We also strictly enforce the Bill's mandate to treat information utility 
records as conclusive evidence to meet the 14-day admissi on window. We also need 
to speed up utilization of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Fund to provide temporary 
funding to stressed companies, protecting jobs and value during the resolution 
process. Also, we need to amend Section 53 to accord MSMEs a fairer priority to 
implement a mandatory minimum payout floor in resolution plans to protect smaller 
suppliers and workers.  We also need to fo rmally codify the code of conduct for CoC 
members, requiring them to act as custod ians of public trust and provide necessary 
day-to-day funding. We must also introd uce simpler procedures, standard formats, 
and guidance support to help MSMEs navi gate pre-pack and other resolution 
options. Project-wise CIRP fo r real estate and infrastruc ture projects need to be 
brought in to prevent stalled projects from jeopardizing the solved ones is also very 
important. Finally, we need to move beyond  procedural coordination towards a legal 
framework for consolidating assets and liabilities in complex group  
insolvencies. … (Time-bell rings.)…  We need to establish clear principles for 
recognizing foreign proceedings to ensure  enabling provisions do not result in 
excessive delegation of legislative power.

suggestions. With these things, while these amendments are a strategic step towards 
upgrading and towards bringing the new ph ase to the IBC, the credibility of our

ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﻩی ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ ﺍﻥ ﻭیٹیﻭﺯیﻑ ﮏѧѧѧѧﺍﮐﻮﻧﺎﻣ ﮉﺭﺱیﺁﻑѧѧѧﻧ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ ﮯѧ ѧ،ﮨ  ﻮںѧѧѧѧﺟﻨﮩ ﮯѧѧѧﻧ ﺍﺱ ﺵیﺩ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮںѧѧѧѧﺍﺭﺑ 
ﮯѧѧѧѧﺭﻭﭘ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﮩﻠ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺎﺋﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻬ ںیﻩ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺁﺝ ﺍﺱ ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧﻻﻧ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺪѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻘﺼ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ یﻩی ﮯѧѧﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﮑ ﻮﺭѧѧѧѧﮐ 
ﺍیﺩ ﺎﺋﮯѧѧѧѧ۔ﺟ 
ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﻣﺠﻬﮯ ﺍﺩی ﮯѧѧﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺟﺐ 2014 ںیﻡ ﮨﻤﺎﺭے ﺮﺍﺋﻢѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘ ﭩﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻨﺴ ﺎﺣﺐѧѧѧﺻ ﻣﻠﮏ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺮﺍﺋﻢѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘ ﭩﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻨﺴ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺑﻨ 
ﻮѧѧѧѧﺗ ﻮںѧѧѧﺍﻧﮩ ﮯѧѧѧﻧ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐﮩ ﺎѧѧѧѧﺗﻬ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ہѧѧѧﻧ ںیﻡ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﮐﻬﺎﺅﻧﮕ ہѧѧ ѧﻧ ﺲѧѧѧѧیﮐ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﻧﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻬ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧ۔ﺩﻭﻧﮕ ﻦیﻝѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺍﺱ ﺖѧѧѧﻭﻗ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﺁﺝ 
ﮏѧѧѧﺗ ﺟﻮ ﮍےѧѧѧѧﺑ ﮍےѧѧѧѧﺑ ﻮﻧﺞѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﭘ ﺖѧѧѧѧیﭘ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺗﻬ ﻮںѧѧѧѧﺟﻨﮩ ﮯѧѧѧﻧ ﺍﺱ ﺵیﺩ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺮﻭڑﻭںѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧﺭﻭﭘ ﻮﭨﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟ ،ںیﻩ ﺟﻦ 
ںیﻡ ﻭﺟﮯ ﻩیﻣﺎﻝ ﻮﺉѧѧѧѧیﮐ 7,500 ﺮﻭڑѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺭﭘﺌ ﺭﻭیﻥ یﻣﻮﺩ 6,400 ﺮﻭڑѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧ،ﺭﻭﭘ ﻞیﻡѧѧﮨ ﻮﮐﺲѧѧѧѧیﭼ 
7,080 ﺮﻭڑѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧ،ﺭﻭﭘ ﺖѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻠ یﻣﻮﺩ 1,700 ﺮﻭڑѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧ،ﺭﻭﭘ یﺍﺱ ﻁﺮﺡ ﻭیﺭﺍﺝ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧ،ﮔﻮﺋ  ﻩیﻭﻥ ﻞѧѧѧѧﻣﺘ ﺭﻩیﻭﻍ ﺟﻮ 
ﻮگѧѧѧﻟ ،ںیﻩ ﻮںѧѧѧﺍﻧﮩ ﮯѧѧѧﻧ ﺁﺝ ﮏѧѧѧﺗ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺟﺘﻨ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ہیپѧѧѧﺳ ،ﺍیﻝ ﺮѧѧѧѧѧﭘﻬ ﻭﻩ ﭩﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﮐﻨ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﺎگѧѧѧѧﺑﻬ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﮔﺌ ﻭﻩ 
ہیپѧѧѧﺳ ﺲѧѧѧѧﻭﺍﭘ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ،ﺍیﺁ ہѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺟﺒﮑ ﻩی ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐﮩ ﺍیگ ﺎѧѧѧѧﺗﻬ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻢѧѧﮨ ﺍﻥ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻭﻩ ہیپѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧѧﻟ ںیﻝ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮔ ﺍﻭﺭ 15-15 
ﻬѧѧѧѧѧѧﻻﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧﺭﻭﭘ ﮔﺎﻭٔں  ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺮѧѧﮨ ﺍٓﺩﻣﯽ ﻮѧѧѧ ѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﮑ ﮏیﺏѧѧﻧ ﺎﺅﻧﭩﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﮐ ںیﻡ ﺩے ﮯیﺩѧѧѧѧѧ۔ﻧﮕ ﻩی ہیپѧѧѧﺳ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ۔ﺍیﺁ 
ﻠﺌﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ ںیﻡ ﺍﺱ ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ یک ﺖѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺯﺑﺮﺩﺳ ﺮﺯﻭﺭѧѧѧѧﭘ ﺖѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻣﺨﺎﻟﻔ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺗ ﻮںѧѧ،ﮨ ہیکѧѧѧѧѧﻭﻧﮑ ﻩی ﻮںیﻏﺮѧѧѧѧﺑ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ 
ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ہѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﻠﮑ ﻩی ہѧѧѧںیﺍﻧ ﻮںѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻮﮔ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺮѧѧѧѧѧﭘﻬ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﭩﺮیﺵѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟ ﮯیﺩѧѧѧﻧ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﮩﻠ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ 
ﺮѧѧѧﺏیﻗ 20 ﻬѧѧѧѧѧѧﻻﮐ ﺮﻭڑѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧﺭﻭﭘ یﺍﺱ ﺎﺏѧѧѧﺣﺴ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻣﻌﺎﻑ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺌ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧﮔﺌ  ںیﻩ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺁﺝ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ یﻩی ﺶѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻮﺷ ﻮѧѧﮨ 
یﺭﻩ ﮯѧѧﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺍﻭﺭ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺟﻮ ﻮگѧѧѧﻟ ،ںیﻩ ﻮѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﮑ یﺍﺱ ﻁﺮﺡ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻮﺭѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧ۔ﻣﻠ ںیﻡ ﻩی ﺎѧѧѧѧѧﭼﺎﮨﺘ ﻮںѧѧﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮںیﻏﺮѧѧѧѧﺑ 
ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ ﻬѧѧѧѧ ѧﮐﭽ ﺍیک ﺎﺋﮯѧѧѧѧﺟ ﺍﻭﺭ ﻮںیﻏﺮѧѧѧѧﺑ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﺱѧѧѧѧﭘ ﺮѧѧѧѧﺍﮔ ﻮڑےѧѧѧѧѧﺗﻬ ﻮڑےѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺗﻬ ﻮﭨﮯѧѧѧѧѧﭼﻬ ﻮﭨﮯѧѧѧѧѧﭼﻬ 
ﺎﺅﻧﭩﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻣ یک ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻩیﺑﻘ یﺩﺍﺭ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﻮѧѧѧѧﺗ ﻮﺍﻥѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻣﻌﺎﻑ ﺍیک ﺎﺋﮯѧѧѧ ѧ۔ﺟ 
ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺮѧѧѧیﺩﻭﺳ ﺎﺕѧѧѧѧ،ﺑ ںیﻡ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﮩﻨ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧﭼﺎﮨﻮﻧﮕ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮨﻤﺎﺭے ﺟﻤﻮں ﻭ ﻢѧѧѧѧѧѧﺭیﮐﺸ ﭧѧѧѧٹیﺍﺳ ںیﻡ کیﺍ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﭘﻨ ﮏیﺏѧѧﻧ 
ﮯѧѧﮨ – ﮉیﺟﮯﺍѧѧѧﻧ  ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮏیﺏѧѧ۔ﻧ ﻩی کیﺍ ﺎﻣﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧﺁﭨﻮﻧ ﮏیﺏѧѧﻧ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﻦیﻝѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﭽﻬﻠ ﺊѧѧѧѧیﮐ ﺎﻟﻮںѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ 
ﮏѧѧیﺍﺳ ﻭﻩ ﺎﻡѧѧѧѧѧѧیﺁﭨﻮﻧ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﭨﻮﭨ ﻢѧѧѧѧﺧﺘ ﻮѧѧﮨ ﺊѧѧѧѧیﮔ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺍﺱ ﮏیﺏѧѧﻧ ںیﻡ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺟﺘﻨ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺎپѧѧѧѧﭨ ﻭﻝیﻝ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ 
ﺮﺱیﺁﻑѧѧѧﺳ ،ںیﻩ ﺟﻮ ﺎپѧѧѧ ѧﭨ  ﺮѧѧѧѧﺍﺱﺑ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﺎﺭﭘﻮﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧѧٹیﮐ ﭩﺮیﺱѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺟﻮ ﻮگѧѧѧﻟ ﮑﻮѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ ﻼѧѧﭼ ﮯѧѧﺭﮨ ،ںیﻩ ﻭﻩ 
ﺎﮨﺮѧѧѧѧѧﺑ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮگѧѧѧﻟ ﻼѧѧﭼ ﮯѧѧﺭﮨ ںیﻩ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺖیﺭѧѧѧﺍﺳ ﺟﻤﻮں ﻭ ﻢѧѧѧѧѧѧﺭیﮐﺸ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮںѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻮﮔ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻭﻩ ﺎﻭﺭѧѧѧѧﭘ یﻥیﭼﻬ ﺊѧѧѧѧی۔ﮔ 
،ﻭﻥیﺍ ﺭﻣﻮٹ ﺯیﺝیﻭﻝ ںیﻡ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺁﺝ ﺁپ ﻬیﺩѧѧѧѧںیﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮔ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ہѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﭩﻨ ﮯѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧﺑﮩ ﮯѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺮﺍﺕѧѧѧѧﮔﺠ ﮯѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺖѧѧѧѧѧﺑﮩ

ﺎﺭѧѧѧیﺳ ﻮںѧѧѧѧﺟﮕﮩ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻮگѧѧѧﻟ ﺁ ﮯѧѧﺭﮨ ،ںیﻩ ﻭے ﺍﺩﻫﺮ ﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍںیﻧﻮﮐ ﺮﺕѧѧѧѧےﮐ ۔ںیﻩ ہѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧﺣﺎﻻﻧﮑ ﻭﻩ ﭼﺎﺭےیﺏ ﺟﺎﻥ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ 
ﻣﺎﺭے ﺮѧѧѧѧﺎﻥیﭘѧѧѧﺷ ،ںیﻩ ہیکѧѧѧѧѧﻭﻧﮑ ﺎﻻﺕѧѧﺣ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﮑ ےیﻝ ﺎѧѧѧﺩیﺷ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺗﻨ ﺎﺯﮔﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ۔ںیﻩ 
ﺮѧѧѧﻞیﺁﻧѧѧѧѧﺑ ﺲѧѧѧѧﻭﺍﺋ ﺮﻡیچѧѧѧѧﻥیﺋ ﺎﺣﺐѧѧѧ،ﺻ ﻣﺠﻬﮯ ﻮѧѧѧѧﺗ 9 ﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻨﭩ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻠﻨ ﮯیﭼﺎﻩѧѧѧѧﺋ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺗﻬ ہیکѧѧѧѧѧﻭﻧﮑ ںیﻡ ﻻیﺍک  
ﻮﻟﻨﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑ ﻭﺍﻻ  ﻮںѧѧ،ﮨ ﻦیﻝѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧﺁﭘﻨ ﻣﺠﻬﮯ ﺮﻑѧѧ ѧﺻ ﻥیﺕ ﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧﻣﻨﭩ ۔،ےیﺩ ﺁپ ﻣﺠﻬﮯ  کیﺍ ﭧѧѧﻣﻨ ﺩے ۔ےیﺝیﺩ

SHRI NIRANJAN BISHI (Odisha) : Thank you, Vice-Chairman, Sir, for giving me the 
opportunity. 
Sir, insolvency frameworks are necessa ry for economic stability. But I ask - 
stability for whom? Relief for whom? And, at what cost? Today, big companies are 
defaulters. But, they restructure loans, ge t legal protection and return stronger. But 
our farmers, tribal communities, poor citizens and MSMEs --they face distress, 
dispossession, and despair. Sir, our farm ers are our backbone and not our burden. 
Farmers are not defaulters by choice. They ar e victims of crop failure, climate change 
and market uncertainty. When a farmer fails, it  is not business failure, it is a failure of 
the system. So, I strongly demand as fo llows: Interest-free and collateral-free loans 
for our farmers and MSMEs, a complete  protective mechanism keeping farmers 
outside harsh insolvency proceedings and a farmer debt relief fr amework with dignity 
and justice. If we can rescue companies, we must protect those who feed our nation. 
Sir, SC/ST, Backward Classes and poor need protection, not pressure. Sir, under 
our Constitutional provisions, Articles 38, 39, and 46,  we are du ty bound to protect 
the Scheduled Castes, the Scheduled Tribes, Backward Classes and weaker

sections. But the ground reality is harsh -- tribal families risk of losing land, poor

Sir, insolvency should not mean punishme nt, it should mean rehabilitation with 
dignity. Let us distinguish between wilful defaulters and distressed citizens.

AFFAIRS (SHRIMATI NIRMALA SITHARAMAN) :  Sir, I am standing here to respond 
to the debate which has been held on the IBC. It is an amendment to the IBC Act of 
2016. Actually, after the Act of 2016 was passed, the World Bank in its 2019 Report 
observed that India's insolvency regime refo rm increased creditor recovery rates from 
26.5 to 71.6 cents on the dollar making India the best performer in South Asia, placing 
it above the average recovery rates of the OECD high income economies. That is how 
the IBC, just after a few years of its introduction, has been recognized the world over. 
Since it is a law which pertains to the economic activity and it has to respond to the 
growing needs of the economy, we have b een undertaking periodic amendments to 
the Act. Six times, we have come to this  House to have it passed for the various 
amendments which were required by the industry, required by the stakeholders. 
Therefore, we have to come for the amen dments. One concrete thing that I can say
