banking sector.  One of the reasons how India's banking sector has actually become 
better in its health is because of the way in which IBC has recovered assets and gone 
through the process and given back money to  the banks. Earlier, they were sitting 
with NPAs. They were not clearly guided by  how to quickly get or restore the value 
and give it off to somebody who can run it and get their dues back. Recoveries 
through the IBC stood at Rs.54,528 crore accounting for 52.3 per cent of all total 
recoveries of Rs.1.04 lakh crore to the banks.  If Rs.1.04 lakh crore were recovered in 
total, through the IBC process, 52.3 per cent was recovered. Periodically, the IBC 
has been facing very many challenges in  terms of the number of benches in the 
NCLT, number of timely filling of vacant positions in NCLT and so on. Despite all 
these challenges, the IBC is pe rforming and performing to bring in value back to the 
economy and making sure that companies are kept going and they are not really 
pushed to liquidation. That is one point which I want to highlight. I will also respond to 
some of the Members. IBC was not brou ght with an intention of liquidating 
companies. It was brought in to address the stress that the companies were facing 
and give a resolution which will make them come back to some form and then attain 
the status in which they were earlier run, wi th quite a few guardrails and ensuring that 
defaulting promoter doesn't come back th rough the back door. It was ensured that 
the resolution would be towards keepin g the company going and not liquidating. 
There are eventually some companies where no resolution is possible in spite of 
repeated attempts; so, they go for liquidation. The intent of the law is not to liquidate 
them, but to give a resolution which will ke ep them going. So, in this current set of 
amendments, we want to bring in expediti ous admission of insolvency applications. 
So, even for admission, if there is time loss, the value of the comp any is getting lost; 
equally, the feeling is, IBC is not delivering quickly. So, by limiting adjudication to the 
existence of default and greater reliance on information ut ilities, we are trying to 
expedite admission of the application itself. Second, statutory timelines for 
adjudicatory authorities to reduce dela ys is being made possible now by 
strengthening the liquidation process throug h enhanced creditor oversight, ensuring 
independence of the liquidator and also maki ng sure removal of procedural overlaps. 
These are the three large pillars through wh ich amendments are coming in here this 
time for the House to consider. 
Sir, one feature of the IBC was the fast  track process, which was brought in 
through an amendment, but it had remained  underutilized. It was not really to the 
extent, to which we thought it would be picked up by the stakeholders. Since it was 
150
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
underutilized, we have now come to replace that fast track process through a 
creditor-initiated insolvency framework and it will feature a few things which are very 
important. One is: Out of court settlements or out of court initiations -- a debtor-in-
possession model wherein the cred itor will have oversight. So, it is not as if this or 
that; both of them will play a role in getti ng a resolution possible, and within defined 
timelines. So, these are the features where, while replacing the fast track mechanism, 
the process that we had brought in through an amendment, with the creditor-initiated 
insolvency framework, we are bringing these features so that the best of both worlds 
can prevail there. It is also during this se t of amendments that we further introduce an 
enabling framework for group in solvency and cross-border insolvency. And, this is 
aimed at improving investor confidence an d aligning domestic processes with the 
international best practices that we know of. 
 Sir, as you know, the Bill was introduced in the Lok Sabha on 12 th August, 
2025. Immediately, it was referred to a Select Committee of the Lok Sabha under the 
Chairmanship of Shri Baijayanta Panda. The Select Committee then presented its 
Report on the 17 th of December, 2025, and it made 11 recommendations. We went 
through the recommendations. In fact, ev en as the Select Committee was going 
through the process, consulting with all the stakeholders, the Ministry of Corporate 
Affairs was actively engaged with the Select Committee, sitting along with them on 
some of the processes and there and then understood where some of the 
recommendations were already featured in some way or the other or it was possible 
to do it through rules. So, there was an active engagement of the Ministry of 
Corporate Affairs with the Select Committ ee. Finally, they gave 11 recommendations. 
We added one more recommendation from Ministry’s side. So, altogether, 12 of them 
have come here. The Select Committee gave us 11 recommendations, which have all 
been accepted. The Select Committee had submitted its Report on 17 th December, 
2025. And after detailed deliberation, on 30th March, the Lok Sabha passed it. 
   Now, I come to specific suggestions and comments made by hon’ble 
Members. There was a lot of concern ex pressed, and rightly so, on the huge 
haircuts, low recovery rates. I want to, firs t of all, reiterate the point that I made a 
minute before. IBC framework is a framework for rescuing viable businesses and 
resolving financial stress while preserving enterprise value. It was never intended to be 
a debt recovery tool. Recovery values are in cidentally a by-product. Therefore, we 
need to understand that the IBC process is largely market-driven, in fact, only 
market-driven. So, the recoveries are re flective of underlying asset quality and 
commercial viability of the distressed enterp rises; various different parameters are 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
151

 
 
looked into and the market de termines what can be the a sset qualities valuation. It 
also depends on firm specific factors, se ctor specific factors and also the larger 
economic considerations. IBC realizes 94.95 pe r cent of the fair value at the time of 
admission. It is important to understand this . So, fair value at the time of admission, 
suppose it has been delayed. After admission, three-four years pass and then there is 
a fair value that you take and which is far infe rior to what it was at that time.  So, at 
the time of admission, what was the fair value, IBC has successfully realized 94.95 
percent of that value. So, realization exceed ing 171.54 percent of the liquidation value 
indicates recovery levels, refl ect the enterprises’ distressed state upon entry, not a 
failure of the resolution framework. We need  to take that on board. As of December, 
2025, IBC has facilitated resolution of 1, 376 companies enabling creditors to recover 
Rs.4.11 lakh crore in total. So, financial creditors have seen recovery exceeding 34 
per cent of their claims. That is the actual stat e of affairs and I want that to be laid in 
the House. 
 There were concerns expressed. I am no t sure if I am able to see the Member 
here, Shri Sukhendu Sekhar Ray, who said that promoters’ re-entry.

Promoters re-entering, Section 29A ge tting diluted was a concern which was 
expressed by the hon’ble Member. I want to emphasize that Section 29A prohibits 
willful defaulters, persons related to the NP As, etc. and their connected parties from 
submitting a resolution plan. In fact, I will tie that up with what hon’ble  Member, Shri 
Rajeev Shukla had mentioned saying why yo u would not have some of the promoters 
come back to take it. In fact, I will answer  that in detail later, but for small firms, 
MSMEs, we do give a chance for the promot er to come and claim if he can provide 
feasible solution. We do accommodate them if  they are small.  But for the big ones; 
‘no’! The defaulting promoter is not allowed to come back. Whatever means that he 
may adopt, we do not entertain that. Ineligib le persons included, and let me put it on 
the table here, who are those who are ine ligible to come back? They are willful 
defaulters, persons related to NPAs, persons against wh om avoidance application 
orders have been made and so on and their connected parties. So, proceedings 
against the corporate debtor halt on succe ssful resolution but proceedings against 
erstwhile promoters will still continue. In other words, if a solution is given through the 
RP and the NCLT has approved it and so on, the new buyer or the new person who is 
taking over the company is not going to be festered with cases.                                                             
 However, the defaulting promoter fo r the omissions and commissions will be 
pursued under law, so that action is taken against him and new business does not get

[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
encumbered or disturbed from settling and growing. In fact, later, when I respond to 
one of the Members, I would highlight the fact  that we are making sure that prior two 
years is also taken on board, so that none  of these cases will be  restored, not from 
the day of the resolution, but even two years before. This is a point which I need to 
explain—it is about the look-back period, an d which is what I was trying to explain—
that the avoidance transactio ns are expanded to two year s before the date on which 
the petition was filed before the adjudicatin g authority, that is, the NCLT. This will 
reduce the incentive for the promoters to delay admission. The Committee of 
Creditors has to record reasons for selectio n of the successful resolution at the end. 
So, this proposed amendment, particularly, has been added after the Select 
Committee’s recommendations. It shows our commitment for transparency. We 
brought it in with full support from our side because it is important to have this entire 
process a lot more transparent so that it does not raise any doubts in the minds of 
people.  Again, Shri Rajiv Shukla  and Shri Ayodhya Rami Reddy  Alla asked about the 
NCLT delays, whether NCLT have functions in  full capacity or not, what are the 
constraints and so on, and about the timeline breaches. The primary cause for 
delay—it is obvious that all of us will accept it and it is fairly open domain 
information—is extensive litigations. Promoters tend to litigate or attempt 
settlements, and delaying admission. As a re sult, you end up with further and further 
delay, not due to the process being stag nated but because there is so much of 
litigation upon litigation. Parallel recovery processes also cost delay.  It is not as if it is 
a clinical situation where the IBC process is set out and it is rolling smoothly, but this 
entire ecosystem works to bring in these handicaps to the IBC. To overcome this, 
following steps have been taken in th e Amendment Bill, which is now under 
consideration. New penalty provisions have  been brought in. There is a penalty on 
persons initiating frivolous, vexatious pr oceedings to prevent abuse and delays. So, 
minimum one lakh rupees or up to two cror e rupees penalties will be levied if the 
initiation is vexatious or frivolous. The Amendment Bill also provides mandatory 14-
day admission timeline. Once the default is established for financial creditors, no 
other criteria is required to be seen. 
Information utility is a very important point because now when they search to 
get credible information, time is spent.  Now, we are very clearly saying that 
information utility maintains the record of the financial information which is required to 
be authenticated by the debtor and this forms the basis of record of default. Based on 
this, NCLT can initiate the process, thus, sa ving time. Sir, the Adjudicating Authority 
will be required to approve or reject resoluti on plan within 30 days of receipt. In fact, 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
153

 
 
this is a little nudge even on the adjudicatin g authority. There is a sensitivity which we 
are conscious of because it looks as if we  are telling the courts to please expedite, 
but it is a gentle nudge. So, I would think it will be appreciated. 
Sir, about Creditor-Initiat ed Insolvency Resolution Process (CIIRP) as they 
would call it now onwards, new out-of-cou rt initiation mechanism with compressed 
150-day timeline is being brought in. So the genuine concerns on NCLT delays, 
capacity restrictions and also the time line breaches are now being taken care of. 
There is this concern which, again, hon’ble Member, Shri Rajeev Shukla, mentioned 
was about workers' dues, operational creditors not prioritized. I would like to highlight 
the point that we are ensuring that the workmen dues are not ignored at all. Under the 
distribution waterfall mechanism, the du es owed to workmen are given a higher 
priority and equivalent to secured creditor s. In fact, even Government dues come 
below the workmen dues. It is above the unsecured financial creditors also. So, we 
are committed to ensuring that the wo rkmen are not short-changed. Hon’ble 
Members, while speaking, did speak ab out the IIM-Ahmedabad study which has 
come out in March, 2026. It did a study on the post-resolution situation. Post-
resolution what happens to the companies? Are they surviving? Are they prospering? 
Are they doing well? So a survey was co nducted and they came up with post-
resolution outcomes showin g that the expenses on employees has increased 72 per 
cent and employee strength per unit of asset increased 200 per cent over five years. 
So, actually, workmen concerns, post-resol ution, are actually being well-attended 
to. If the company was waiting for resolution, its value is decaying, it would have not 
done any good to the workers. Whereas, once the resolution has happened, the new 
bidder has taken the company over and it is  running it, he is spending more on 
workmen and he is also making sure th at there are more number of workmen per 
asset unit. This is a very good encouraging sign of the IBC process. 
On MSME and distress of MSMEs, pr e-pack for MSMEs, some concern was

in rescuing their business to ensure small pl ayers are not made to lose their business 
which gets into insolvency. In 2021, when we brought in the Sixth Amendment to the 
Code, the prepackaged insolvency resolution process was introduced for corporate 
MSMEs to fast-track resolution of cases for MSMEs. So, even the voting threshold 
was brought down. From 66 per cent, I think, it was brought down to 51 percent and 
also the documentation pr ocesses would be reduced.  So, compliance--particularly, 
when you are looking at activities, which ar e PUFE activities, issuing of preferential

 Sir, this concern was expressed. This  Amendment Bill provides for the Central 
Government to make rules for group insolv ency under Clause 42 and cross-border 
insolvency under Clause 67. These two Clauses contain provisions for sufficient 
Parliamentary oversight. There are quite a few safeguards to tackle concerns of 
excessive delegation. For instance, the basi c tenets of the different frameworks like 
cross-border insolvency are directly provided within the primary law, the Code itself, 
rather than leaving it entirely to the rule s. The draft rules are required to be placed 
before Parliament before implementation , with any modification or disapproval 
possible. So, that is another avenue or oppo rtunity for Parliamentary oversight on this 
matter. 
The concerns regarding excessive delegation were also noted by the Select

now come here, has been modified to assuag e any concern in this regard. Is there a

CoC can actually direct the resolution profe ssional to invite project-wise plans, or a 
separate plan for each tower if there is a group of towers. The resolution professional,

given possession to an allottee in a real estate project, such asset shall not form a

authorised representatives and facilitators, who can participate in the meetings of the 
Committee of Creditors for such subclasses wi thin large creditor classes — such as 
home buyers — to ensure their effective participation, are also parts of this insolvency 
resolution process.

Shrimati Priyanka Chaturvedi. 
Concern over invisible victims of India's correctional system 
SHRIMATI PRIYANKA CHATURVEDI (Maharashtra) : S i r ,  I  r i s e  t o  s p e a k  o n  t h e  
‘Concern over Invisible Victims of Indi a's Correctional System.’ Children of 
incarcerated parents remain among the most invisible groups in India’s justice 
system. Despite their vulnerability, there is  no reliable national data on how many 
such children exist, their liv ing conditions, or their acce ss to education and welfare. 
Even within prisons, ba sic facilities such as cr èches and childcare support are either 
inadequate or absent, while children outside face stigma, disruption in schooling, and 
economic distress. According to the Nati onal Crime Records Bureau (NCRB), the 
study notes that as of December 2023, a to tal of 1,492 children were living inside 
Indian prisons, with 1,318 women. Since prisons and law and justice fall under the 
State List, there is no uniform national fr amework, no standardised data collection, 
and no minimum benchmarks of care for these children. As a result, States follow 
fragmented approaches, and these children re main outside the focus of both justice 
and welfare systems. Prison data in India does not capture the long-term impact on 
children, nor does it track outcomes post-i ncarceration. The absence of data itself 
reflects the absence of policy attention. 
I urge the Government to develop a uniform policy framework with minimum 
standards of care. Additionally, establis h a national database of children of 
incarcerated parents, and ensure coordi nation between prison administration, 
education systems, and child protection servi ces so that these children are not left 
behind. Thank you so much, Sir.

Demand for creation of an independent Postal Circle for Goa 
SHRI SADANAND MHALU SHET TANAVADE (Goa) : Sir, I seek to draw attention of 
the Government to a serious administrative issue concerning postal administration in 
Goa. At present, postal services in Go a are administered under the Maharashtra 
Postal Circle. Despite being a separate State with its own distinct identity, Goa 
continues to remain under the administrative control of this circle. While this 
arrangement may have been suitable earlier , it no longer meets present needs, as 
Goa has its own administrative, linguistic and socio-economic requirements. This 
continued dependence on another State’s ad ministrative structure has led to several 
practical challenges. Firstly, recruitment to postal services is conducted at the circle 
level. As a result, candidates from larger  States tend to dominate the selection 
process due to greater competition, thereby limiting fair employment opportunities for 
164
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
local youth in Goa and reducing their repr esentation in public service delivery.  
Secondly, many employees recruited from ou tside Goa seek transfers back to their 
parent States after a short tenure, leadin g to recurring vacancies. These vacancies 
are often managed through temporary arrangements, affecting continuity and 
efficiency in postal services. Goa’s small ge ographical size should not be seen as a 
constraint, but rather as an advantage in  creating a more responsive, efficient and 
citizen-centric postal administration. 
I, therefore, urge the Government to co nsider the creation of an independent 
postal circle for Goa. This step will ensu re better administrative control, improve 
service delivery and enhance employment opportunities for local youth in Goa.

Dr. Medha Vishram Kulkarni. 
Concerns regarding unfair rejection and delay in health insurance claim  
settlements by private insurance companies 
DR. MEDHA VISHRAM KULKARNI (Maharashtra): Sir, I seek to draw the attention of 
the Government to the growing concern re garding the increasing instances of delay 
and unjust rejection of health insuran ce claims by certain private insurance 
companies, causing serious hardship to policyholders across the country. Private 
insurers promote health policies with pr omises of cashless treatment and quick 
settlement of claims. Yet, during medical emergencies many families later find their 
claims partially approved, delayed, or re jected on technical grounds such as pre-
existing disease clauses, documentation issues, or restrictive policy conditions. 
According to the latest annual report of the Insurance Regulatory and Development 
Authority of India, nearly 2.57 lakh complain ts were registered in the insurance sector 
during 2024-25, including about 1.22 lakh related to claim settlement disputes, a large 
share from health insurance. Policyholders have also raised concerns regarding the 
practices of certain insurers where de lays, repeated document requests and 
prolonged verification processes create difficult ies for patients and hospitals. In many 
[ 01 April, 2026 ]
165

cases, patients enter hospitals expecting insurance coverage, but at the time of 
discharge, families have to arrange large payments due to unresolved claims, placing 
severe financial and emotional pressure on ordinary citizens. 
I, therefore, urge the Government to strengthen regulatory oversight, ensure 
strict action against insurers unfairly rejecting claims, mandate dedicated claim-
settlement managers to coordinate with hosp itals, require transparent disclosure of 
policy terms before sale and ensure that a ll insurance-related complaints are resolved 
within a fixed time frame of 30 days to protect policyholders and maintain public 
confidence in the health insurance system.
